      minhash: PQzfo+GOa5glLP9sHA+noHcj9EHObuwxmYWrsF9q5RY7DVlMJ40OyLEYbRS5fl3q3h9CZCDLQZJ0zP0KkvycHOAHd/I/T/f6SHn707qDDXRMte/VY6pjQzVYAfVhmbbUJ70tKBkeAO8GXeBJZBBbdRcuLz5GtgECXnU4cT2rk+Lz+7kmXaZizjgG99Ag/gETfYb+uA0ZzmFqy8WzEuiMj49rilKMlWQFfjgWrgwI/rmJLt2mhw7h6n8YeKCifjxgOHybcGGVP6A5TAXsW/ZFde9VpMQC2QNEuhe170ZGi9eZXQFcFHbIMZLCe8hXNsDqWEI/Gi+7b6rE3Uim+urYzg==

<!-- defin-1: body_md -->
We say that a function $q:[0,T] \times E \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ is a local Carath\'{e}odory solution of (\ref{eq:Cauchyprob}) if and only if
	\begin{itemize}
		\item[$(i)$] $q \in \operatorname{\mathcal{B}}([0,T]\times E)$, $q(\cdot,x) \in C[0,T]$ for any $x \in E$ and $q(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}$ for any $t \in (0,T]$;
		\item[$(ii)$] $q(0,x)=u(x)$ for any $x \in E$;
		\item[$(iii)$] $Gq(\cdot,x) \in L^1(0,T)$ for any $x \in E$;
		\item[$(iv)$] For any $x \in E$ and $t \ge 0$
		\begin{equation}\label{eq:globCara}
			q(t,x)=u(x)+\int_0^t Gq(s,x)ds.
		\end{equation}
	\end{itemize}
	We say that $q:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ is a global Carath\'{e}odory solution of (\ref{eq:Cauchyprob}) if its restriction to $[0,T]$ is a local Carath\'{e}odory solution for any $T>0$.
<!-- end defin-1 -->
<!-- rmk-1: body_md -->
This definition is related to the so-called mild solution of an abstract Cauchy problem (see \cite[Definition 3.1.1]{arendt2011vector}). For instance, suppose that $G$ is a closed linear operator on $\mathcal{C} \subset C_0  \left(  E \right) $. Assume that $q$ is a global Carath\'eodory solution of (\ref{eq:Cauchyprob}) such that $Gq(t,\cdot) \in C_0(E)$ and $t \in [0,T] \mapsto \left\Vert Gq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C_0(E)} \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ belongs to $L^1[0,T]$ for all $T>0$. Notice also that, by definition of Carath\'eodory solution, $q(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}$. Hence, {for convenience, we can denote} $q(t)=q(t,\cdot)$ so that $q:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \to C_0(E)$. Then, in particular, by \cite[Proposition 1.1.7]{arendt2011vector}, we have
    \begin{align*}
			\int_0^t Gq(s) ds \, = \, G \int_0^t q(s) ds.
    \end{align*}
                         
    If also $u \in C_0(E)$, we can rewrite (\ref{eq:globCara}) in terms of Bochner integrals as
    \begin{equation*}
 		q(t)=u+G\int_0^t q(s)ds,
    \end{equation*}
    i.e., $q$ is a mild solution of the abstract Cauchy problem in $C_0(E)$
    \begin{equation*}
		\begin{cases}
			\partial_t q(t)=Gq(t), & t>0, \\[7pt]
			q(0)=u.
		\end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end rmk-1 -->
<!-- defin-2: body_md -->
We say that the couple $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$, where $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0 \subseteq \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$ and $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol} \subseteq \operatorname{\mathcal{B}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E)$ is a \textit{uniqueness class} for $G$ if and only if
	\begin{itemize}
		\item[$(i)$] For any $q \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}$ and any $t>0$ it holds $q(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0 \cap \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}$;
		\item[$(ii)$] For any $u \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ there exists a unique global Carath\'{e}odory solution $q \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}$ to (\ref{eq:Cauchyprob}).
	\end{itemize}
<!-- end defin-2 -->
<!-- ex-1: body_md -->
Consider $E=\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$, $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}=C^2(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$ and $G=\partial^2_x$. If we set $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0=C_0(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$ and 
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}=\{q \in C(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})\cap C^1(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}): \ q(t,\cdot) \in C^2_0(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}), \ \forall t>0, \mbox{ and } q(0,\cdot) \in C_0(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})\},
\end{equation*}
then $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ is a uniqueness class for $G$, as it is well-known that the heat equation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:heateq}
	\begin{cases}
		\partial_t q(t,x)=\partial^2_x q(t,x), & t >0, x \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}},\\
		q(0,x)=u(x), & x \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}},
	\end{cases}
\end{equation}
admits a unique solution in $C^2_0(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$ if $u\in C_0(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$. It is important to notice that such a solution is not unique among all the measurable functions on $\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$, as we could find other unbounded solutions of the same equation, that are usually called non-physical solutions (see \cite[Theorem 2.3.1]{cannon1984one}).
<!-- end ex-1 -->
<!-- ex-2: body_md -->
Again, consider $E=\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$, $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}=C^2(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$ and $G=\partial^2_x$. Now set 
	\begin{equation*}
		\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0=\{u \in C(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}): \ \exists c_1,c_2>0, \ \exists \gamma \in [0,2), \ |u(x)| \le c_1e^{c_2|x|^\gamma}, \ \forall x \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}\}
	\end{equation*}
	and
	\begin{multline*}
		\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}=\{q \in C(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}) \cap C^1(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}): \ q(t,\cdot) \in C^2(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}), \ \forall t>0,\\ \mbox{ and } \ \forall T>0,  \exists c_1,c_2>0, \exists \gamma \in [0,2), \ |q(t,x)| \le c_1e^{c_2|x|^\gamma},\\ \ \forall x \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}, \ \forall t \in [0,T]\}.
	\end{multline*}
	 Then $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ is a uniqueness class for $G$ as (\ref{eq:heateq}) admits a unique solution $q \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}$ whenever $u \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ (see \cite[Theorem 3.6.1]{cannon1984one}). Let us stress that we could also consider $G$ as the generator of the Brownian motion. In such a case, however, since $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{0} \not \subset C_0(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$, the usual theory of Feller semigroups (see \cite[Definition III.6.5]{rogers2000diffusions}) does not apply.
<!-- end ex-2 -->
<!-- ex-3: body_md -->
Now let $E=[a,b] \subset \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ for some $a<b$. As before, set $G=\partial^2_x$ with $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}=C^2(a,b)$. Set 
	\begin{align*}
	\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0&=\{u \in C[a,b]: \ u(a)=u(b)=0\}\\
	\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}&=\{q \in C(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times [a,b]) \cap C^2(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times (a,b)): \ q(t,a)=q(t,b)=0, \ \forall t > 0\}.
	\end{align*}
	Then $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ is a uniqueness class for $G$. Indeed, in such a case, $q \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}$ is a solution of (\ref{eq:Cauchyprob}) if and only if it solves
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{cases}
			\partial_t q(t,x)=\partial^2_x q(t,x), & t>0, \ x\in (a,b)\\
			q(0,x)=u(x) & x \in [a,b] \\
			q(t,a)=q(t,b)=0 & t>0,
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation*}
	that is a Dirichlet-Cauchy problem and then admits a unique solution.
<!-- end ex-3 -->
<!-- ex-4: body_md -->
\label{ex:Feller}
	Let now $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ be a Feller semigroup on $C_0(E)$ with generator $(G,{\rm Dom}(G))$. Then we can set $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0=C_0(E)$ and $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}= C(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+;C_0 \left( E \right) )$. By standard Feller semigroups theory (see \cite[Chapter 3]{arendt2011vector}), we know that $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ is a uniqueness class for $G$. This implies that the abstract setting we are considering can be applied in general to Feller semigroups and their generators. We can also consider strong solutions in this framework by setting $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0 = {\rm Dom}(G)$ and $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol} = C^1 \left( \mathbb{R}_0^+ ; {\rm Dom}(G) \right)$.
<!-- end ex-4 -->
<!-- defin-3: body_md -->
\label{def:semigroupaction}
For a linear operator $G:\mathcal{C} \subseteq \operatorname{\mathcal{E}} \to \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$ and a uniqueness class $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ for $G$, we define the family of linear operators $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ acting on $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ as follows:
\begin{itemize}
	\item[$(i)$] $P_0=I$, where $I$ is the identity on $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$;
	\item[$(ii)$] For any $t>0$, $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ and $x \in \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$, $P_tu(x)=q(t,x)$, where $q$ is the unique Carath\'{e}odory solution of (\ref{eq:Cauchyprob}) in $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}$.
\end{itemize}
We call the family $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ the semigroup action induced by $G$ on $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$.
<!-- end defin-3 -->
<!-- defin-4: body_md -->
A function $\phi:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+_0$ is said to be a Bernstein function if, $\phi (\lambda) \geq 0$, $\phi \in C^\infty(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+)$ and for any $n \in \operatorname{\mathbb{N}}$
	\begin{equation*}
		(-1)^{n-1}\phi^{(n)}(\lambda) \ge 0 \quad \forall \lambda \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+.
	\end{equation*}
	We denote by $\mathfrak{B}$ the convex cone of Bernstein functions.
<!-- end defin-4 -->
<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
For any $\phi \in \mathfrak{B}$ there exists a unique triplet $(a_\phi,b_\phi,\nu_\phi)$, where $a_\phi,b_\phi \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+$ and $\nu_\phi$ is a Borel measure on $(0,+\infty)$ with the property
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Levydef}
		\int_{(0,\infty)}(1 \wedge t)\, \nu_\phi(dt)<\infty,
	\end{equation}
	such that
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Bdef}
		\phi(\lambda)=a_\phi+b_\phi \lambda +\int_{(0,\infty)}(1-e^{-\lambda t})\nu_\phi(dt).
	\end{equation}
	Vice versa, any triplet $(a_\phi,b_\phi,\nu_\phi)$ with $a_\phi,b_\phi>0$ and $\nu_\phi$ a Borel measure on $(0,+\infty)$ satisfying (\ref{eq:Levydef}) defines a Bernstein function $\phi$ by means of (\ref{eq:Bdef}).
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- defin-5: body_md -->
The triplet $(a_\phi,b_\phi,\nu_\phi)$ is called the L\'evy triplet of $\phi$ and, in particular, $\nu_\phi$ is called the L\'evy measure of $\phi$. We denote by $\mathfrak{B}_0$ the subset of $\mathfrak{B}$ composed of Bernstein functions with triplet $(0,0,\nu_\phi)$ and such that $\nu_\phi(0,\infty)=\infty$.
<!-- end defin-5 -->
<!-- defin-6: body_md -->
Consider a linear operator $G:\operatorname{\mathcal{C}} \subseteq \operatorname{\mathcal{E}} \to \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$, a uniqueness class $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ for $G$ and its induced semigroup action $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ on $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$. Let $\phi \in \mathfrak{B}_0$ and $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{B}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E)$ such that, for any $t \ge 0$, $f(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$. For $t>0$ and $x \in E$ we define 
\begin{equation*}
	-\phi\left(\partial_t-G\right)f(t,x)=\int_0^{+\infty}(P_{s}f(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)-f(t,x))\nu_\phi(ds),
\end{equation*}
	where, for any $t<0$, we set $f(t,x)\equiv 0$, provided the integral is well-defined.
<!-- end defin-6 -->
<!-- thm-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:integrability2}
	Let $G:\operatorname{\mathcal{C}} \subseteq \operatorname{\mathcal{E}} \to \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$ be a linear operator, $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0, \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ be a uniqueness class for $G$ and $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ be the semigroup action induced by $G$ on $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$. Let also $\phi \in \mathfrak{B}_0$. Consider a function $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{B}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E)$ such that $f(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$. Fix $(t,x) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E$ and assume further that:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item[$(i)$] It holds 
            \begin{equation*}
                \int_0^t|P_s {f}(t-s,x)-P_s {f}(t,x)|\nu_\phi(ds)<\infty; 
            \end{equation*}
            \item[$(ii)$]
                It holds
            \begin{equation*}
                \int_0^t|P_s {f}(t,x)-{f}(t,x)|\nu_\phi(ds)<\infty .
            \end{equation*}
	\end{itemize}
	Then $-\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t,x)$ is well-defined.
<!-- end thm-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
It is sufficient to split the integral defining $-\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t,x)$ as follows:
    \begin{align*}
    -\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t,x)&=\int_0^t (P_sf(t-s,x)-P_sf(t,x))\nu_\phi(ds)\\
    &+\int_0^t (P_sf(t,x)-f(t,x))\nu_\phi(ds)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)f(t,x),
    \end{align*}
    where the two integrals are absolutely convergent by $(i)$ and $(ii)$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:Lapinside2}
	Let $G:\operatorname{\mathcal{C}} \subseteq \operatorname{\mathcal{E}} \to \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$ be a linear operator, $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0, \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ be a uniqueness class for $G$ and $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ be the semigroup action induced by $G$ on $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$. Let also $\phi \in \mathfrak{B}_0$. Consider a function $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{B}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E)$ such that $f(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$. Fix $x \in E$ and suppose that the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:integrability2} hold on $(t,x)$ for a.a. $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+$. Assume further that
	\begin{itemize}
		\item[$(i)$] There exists $\lambda_0>0$ such that for all $\lambda>\lambda_0$
            \begin{equation*}
                \int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_0^t|P_s {f}(t-s,x)-P_s {f}(t,x)|\,\nu_\phi(ds)\, dt<\infty ;
            \end{equation*}
            \item[$(ii)$]
                There exists $\lambda_1>0$ such that for all $\lambda>\lambda_1$
            \begin{equation*}
                \int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_0^t|P_s{f}(t,x)-{f}(t,x)|\, \nu_\phi(ds)\, dt<\infty ;
            \end{equation*}
		\item[$(iii)$]
                There exists $\lambda_2>0$ such that for all $\lambda>\lambda_2$
            \begin{equation*}
                \int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)|{f}(t,x)|\,  dt<\infty .
            \end{equation*}
	\end{itemize}
	Then, setting $\lambda_\star:=\max\{\lambda_0,\lambda_1,\lambda_2\}$, for all $\lambda>\lambda_\star$ it holds
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Laptransex}
    \int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}|(-\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t,x))|dt <\infty
    \end{equation}
    and
\begin{align}\label{eq:postFubini}
		\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}(-\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t,x))dt
  =\int_0^\infty \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}(P_sf(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)-f(t,x)) dt\, \nu_\phi(ds).
	\end{align}
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- defin-7: body_md -->
We say that the semigroup action $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ is positivity preserving if for all $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ with $f(x) \ge 0$ for all $x \in E$ it holds $P_tf(x) \ge 0$ for all $x \in E$ and $t \ge 0$.

    We say that the semigroup action $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ is sub-Markov if there exists a one-parameter semigroup of operators $(P^{\rm b}_t)_{t \ge 0}$ acting on $C_{\rm b}(E)$ and coinciding with $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ on $\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0 \cap C_{\rm b}(E)$ such that $P^{\rm b}_t1 \le 1$.
<!-- end defin-7 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:maxprin}
    Assume that the semigroup action $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ is positivity preserving and sub-Markov.

                                                                                           
    
    
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    
  \begin{itemize}
    \item[$(i)$] Assume there exists $(t_\star,x_\star) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times E$ such that $f(t_\star,x_\star) \ge f(t,x)$ for all $(t,x) \in (0,t_\star] \times E$ and $f(t_\star,x_\star) > f(0,x)$, for all $x \in E$ and $-\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t_\star,x_\star)$ is well-defined. Then 
    \begin{equation*}
    -\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t_\star,x_\star)+\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_\star)f(0,x_\star)<0.
    \end{equation*}
    \item[$(ii)$] Assume there exists $(t_\star,x_\star) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times E$ such that $f(t_\star,x_\star) \le f(t,x)$ for all $(t,x) \in (0,t_\star] \times E$, $f(t_\star,x_\star) < f(0,x)$ for all $x \in E$  and $-\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t_\star,x_\star)$ is well-defined. Then 
    \begin{equation*}
    -\phi(\partial_t-G)f(t_\star,x_\star)+\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_\star)f(0,x_\star)>0.
    \end{equation*}
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We just prove $(i)$, since the proof of $(ii)$ is analogous. For $t \in [0,t_\star]$ and $x \in E$ define $\overline{f}(t,x)=f(t_\star,x_\star)-f(t,x) \ge 0$. Since $P_t$ is positivity preserving, we have $P_s\overline{f}(t_\star-s,x) \ge 0$ for all $s \in [0,t_\star]$ and $x \in E$, that in turn implies $P_s f(t_\star-s,x) \le (P_s f(t_\star,x_\star))(x)$ for all $s \in [0,t_\star]$ and $x \in E$. Furthermore, since $P_s$ is sub-Markov and linear, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        P_s f(t_\star-s,x)\le f(t_\star,x_\star),  \ \forall s \in [0,t_\star] \ \forall x \in E.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, it is sufficient to observe that
    \begin{align*}
        -\phi(\partial_t-G)&f(t_\star,x_\star)+\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_\star)f(0,x_\star)\\
        &=\int_0^{t_\star}(P_s f(t_\star-s,x_\star)-f(t_\star,x_\star))\nu_\phi(ds)\\
        &\quad +\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_\star)(f(0,x_\star)-f(t_\star,x_\star))<0.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- defin-8: body_md -->
\label{def:sol}
    We say that $q:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ is a solution of (\ref{eq:q}) if  $q$ is continuous, $\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)$ is well-defined for all $(t,x) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times E$ and the equality in (\ref{eq:q}) hold.
<!-- end defin-8 -->
<!-- defin-9: body_md -->
Consider a probability space $(\Omega, \operatorname{\mathcal{F}}_\infty, \mathds{P})$ and a filtration $\operatorname{\mathcal{F}}:=\{\mathcal{F}_t\}_{t \ge 0}$ on it. An $E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^m$-valued stochastic process $(X,Y)$ is said to be Markov additive with respect to $\operatorname{\mathcal{F}}$ if it is $\operatorname{\mathcal{F}}$-Markov and for all $A \in \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$, $B \in \operatorname{\mathcal{B}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^m)$ and $0 \le s \le t$
    \begin{align*}
        P_{s,t}\mathds{1}_{A \times B}(x,y)=P_{s,t}\mathds{1}_{A \times (B-y)}(x,0),
    \end{align*}
    where $B-y=\{z \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^m: \ z+y \in B\}$ and $P_{s,t}$ is the two-parameters transition semigroup of $(X,Y)$. Notice that the latter is equivalent to
    \begin{align}\label{eq:Markovadd}
    \begin{split}
        \mathds{P}&((X(t),Y(t)-Y(s)) \in A \times B \mid X(s),Y(s))\\
        &=\mathds{P}((X(t),Y(t)-Y(s)) \in A \times B \mid X(s)).       
    \end{split}
    \end{align}
<!-- end defin-9 -->
<!-- lem-1: body_md -->
\label{markovadd}
    The process $(M^\phi,S)$ is Markov additive with respect to its augmented natural filtration $\operatorname{\mathcal{G}}$.
<!-- end lem-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
We already know that $(M^\phi,S)$ is a Feller process with respect to $\operatorname{\mathcal{G}}$, hence we just need to show (\ref{eq:Markovadd}). Let $s < t$, $A \in \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$ and $B \in \operatorname{\mathcal{B}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$, {let $M^\phi (t)$ play the role of $X(t)$ and $S(t)$ play the role of $Y(t)$} and observe that, setting $\Delta S=S(t)-S(s)$,
	\begin{align*}
		&\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}  \left(  (M^\phi(t),\Delta S) \in A \times B \mid M^\phi(s), S (s)  \right)  \notag \\ 
		= \,  & \mathds{E}^{(x,v)} \left[ \mathds{E}^{(x,v)}  \left[\mathds{1}_A  \left(  M^\phi(t) \right)  \mathds{1}_B  \left(  \Delta S \right)  \mid M^\phi(s), S(s),\Delta S \right] \mid M^\phi(s), S(s) \right] \notag \\
		= \, &\mathds{E}^{(x,v)} \left[ \mathds{1}_B  \left(  \Delta S \right)  \mathds{E}^{(x,v)}  \left[\mathds{1}_A  \left(  M^\phi(t) \right)   \mid M^\phi(s), S(s), \Delta S \right] \mid M^\phi(s), S(s) \right] \notag 
  \end{align*}
    Now recall that $M^\phi(t)=M(S(t)-v)=M(\Delta S+S(s)-v)$ and $M^\phi(s)=M(S(s)-v)$, where $S(s)<S(t)$. Now consider three Borel sets $B_1 \in \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$, $B_2,B_3 \subset \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ and the event
    \begin{equation*}
    B^\star=\{M^\phi(s) \in B_1\} \cap \{S(s) \in B_2\} \cap \{\Delta S \in B_3\} \in \sigma(M^\phi(s),S(s),\Delta S).
    \end{equation*}
    It holds
    \begin{align*}
        &\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{B^\star}\mathds{1}_A (M^\phi(t)) ]\\
        &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{B_1}(M^\phi(s))\mathds{1}_{B_2}(S(s))\mathds{1}_{B_3}(\Delta S)\mathds{1}_A (M(\Delta S+S(s)-v)) ]\\
        &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_3}(\Delta S)\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_1}(M^\phi(s))\mathds{1}_{B_2}(S(s))\mathds{1}_A (M(\Delta S+S(s)-v))\mid \Delta S\right]\right].
    \end{align*}
    Once we notice that $\{M(t), \ t \ge 0\}$ and $S(s)$ are independent of $\Delta S$, if we set
    \begin{equation*}
        F_1(y)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_1}(M^\phi(s))\mathds{1}_{B_2}(S(s))\mathds{1}_A (M(y+S(s)-v))\right],
    \end{equation*}
    it holds
    \begin{equation*}
        \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{B^\star}\mathds{1}_A (M^\phi(t)) ]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_3}(\Delta S)F_1(\Delta S)\right].
    \end{equation*}
    However, we can also write
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:condition1}
    F_1(y)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_1}(M^\phi(s))\mathds{1}_{B_2}(S(s))\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_A (M(y+S(s)-v))\mid M^\phi(s), S(s)\right]\right].
    \end{equation}
    Let us focus on the inner conditional expectation. Consider again two sets $B_4 \in \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$ and $B_5 \in \operatorname{\mathcal{E}}$ and the event
    \begin{equation*}
        B_{\star \star}=\{M^\phi(s) \in B_4\} \cap \{S(s) \in B_5\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Then
    \begin{align*}
        \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}&\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{\star \star}} \mathds{1}_A(M(y+{S}(s)-v)\right]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_4}(M^\phi(s))\mathds{1}_{B_5}({S}(s)) \mathds{1}_A(M(y+{S}(s)-v)\right]\\
        &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_5}(S(s))\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_4}(M^\phi(s)) \mathds{1}_A(M(y+S(s)-v)\mid S(s)\right] \right].
    \end{align*}
    Since $M$ and ${S}$ are independent, if we set, for $z \ge v$,
    \begin{align*}
        F_2(z)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_4}(M(z-v)) \mathds{1}_A(M(y+z-v)\right],
    \end{align*}
    it holds
    \begin{equation*}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{\star \star}} \mathds{1}_A(M(y+S(s)-v)\right]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_5}(S(s))F_2(S(s))\right].
    \end{equation*}
    However, since $\{M(z-v) \in B_4\} \in \sigma(M(z-v))$, we have
    \begin{align*}
    F_2(z)&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_4}(M(z-v)) \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_A(M(y+z-v) \mid M(z-v)\right]\right]\\
    &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_4}(M(z-v)) \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(M(z-v),v)}\left[\mathds{1}_A(M(y))\right]\right].
    \end{align*}
    Hence
    \begin{align*}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}&\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{\star \star}} \mathds{1}_A(M(y+S(s)-v)\right]\\&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_5}(S(s))\mathds{1}_{B_4}(M^\phi(s))\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(M^\phi(s),v)}\left[\mathds{1}_A(M(y))\right]\right]\\
    &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_{\star\star}}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(M^\phi(s),v)}\left[\mathds{1}_A(M(y))\right]\right].
    \end{align*}
    Since the events of the form $B_{\star\star}$ constitute a $\pi$-system generating $\sigma(M^\phi(s),S(s))$, this shows that
    \begin{equation*}
        \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_A (M(y+S(s)-v))\mid M^\phi(s), S(s)\right]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(M^\phi(s),v)}\left[\mathds{1}_A(M(y))\right].
    \end{equation*}
    Define
    \begin{equation*}
        F_3(x,y)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_A(M(y))\right]
    \end{equation*}
    so that going back to (\ref{eq:condition1}) we get
    \begin{equation*}
        F_1(y)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B_1}(M^\phi(s))\mathds{1}_{B_2}(S(s))F_3(M^\phi(s),\Delta S)\right]
    \end{equation*}
    and then
    \begin{equation*}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{B^\star}\mathds{1}_A (M^\phi(t)) ]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{B^\star}F_3(M^\phi(s),\Delta S)\right],
    \end{equation*}
    i.e., since the events of the form $B^\star$ constitute a $\pi$-system generating $\sigma(M^\phi(s),S(s),\Delta S)$,
    \begin{equation*}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_A (M^\phi(t))\mid M^\phi(s),S(s),\Delta S]=F_3(M^\phi(s),\Delta S).
    \end{equation*}
    Since $\Delta S$ is independent of both $M^\phi(s)$ and $S(s)$, we get (see Lemma \ref{lem:condexpprop})
    \begin{equation}
        \mathds{P}^{(x,v)}((M^\phi(t),\Delta S) \in A \times B \mid M^\phi(s), S(s))=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_B(\Delta {S})F_3(M^\phi(s),\Delta S) \mid M^\phi(s)\right].
        \label{propmedcond}
    \end{equation}
    Use now (\ref{propmedcond}) to see that
    \begin{align*}
    \mathds{P}^{(x,v)}&((M^\phi(t),\Delta S) \in A \times B \mid M^\phi(s))\\
    &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_B(\Delta S)\mathds{1}_{A}(M^\phi({t})) \mid M^\phi(s)\right]\\
    &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_B(\Delta S)\mathds{1}_{A}(M^\phi({t}))\mid M^\phi(s),S(s)\right] \mid M^\phi(s)\right]\\
    &{=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[ \mathds{P}^{(x,v)}((M^\phi(t),\Delta S) \in A \times B \mid M^\phi(s), S(s)) \mid M^\phi(s) \right]}\\
    &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_B(\Delta S)F_3(M^\phi(s),\Delta S) \mid M^\phi(s)\right] \mid M^\phi(s)\right]\\
    &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_B(\Delta S)F_3(M^\phi(s),\Delta S) \mid M^\phi(s)\right]\\
    &=\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}((M^\phi(t),\Delta S) \in A \times B \mid M^\phi(s),S(s)). \qedhere
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- defin-10: body_md -->
Let $u:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ and $T>0$. We say that 
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{x \to \infty}u(t,x)=0 \quad \mbox{ uniformly with respect to }t \in [0,T]
\end{equation*}
if for all $\varepsilon>0$ there exists a compact set $K \subset E$ such that
\begin{equation*}
    \sup_{x \not \in K}|u(t,x)|<\varepsilon, \quad \forall t \in [0,T].
\end{equation*}
We say that
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{x \to \infty}u(t,x)=0 \quad \mbox{ locally uniformly with respect to }t \ge 0
\end{equation*}
if the same property holds uniformly with respect to $t \in [0,T]$ for all $T>0$.
<!-- end defin-10 -->
<!-- thm-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:main1}
	Let $\phi \in \mathfrak{SB}_0$ such that $\left\Vert \log(\cdot)u^\phi(\cdot) \right\Vert_{L^1[0,1]}<\infty$ and $M$ be a $E$-valued Feller process with semigroup $\{P_t\}_{t \ge 0}$ and generator $(G,{\sf Dom}(G))$. Let also $S$ be a real-valued, strictly increasing c\`adl\`ag process with independent and stationary increments. Assume further that $S$ is independent of $M$ and that the subordinator $S_0(t)=S(t)-S(0)$ is such that $\phi(\lambda)=\log\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[e^{-\lambda S_0(1)}]$. Let $H$ be the undershooting of $S$ and $X(t)=M(H(t))$. Finally, suppose that $S_0(t)$ admits a density {$g_{S_0}(\cdot;t)$} under $\mathds{P}^{(x,0)}$. Then, for all $u \in {\sf Dom}(G)$, the function
    \begin{equation*}
    q(t,x)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[u(X(t))]
    \end{equation*}
    is the unique solution of
    	\begin{equation}\label{eq:main1}
		\begin{cases}
		\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(0,x), &  t>0, \ x \in E,\\
		q(0,x)=u(x), & x \in E\\
            \lim_{x \to \infty}q(t,x)=0 & \mbox{locally uniformly with respect to }t \ge 0,
		\end{cases}
        	\end{equation}
            i.e., it is the unique function $q: \mathbb{R}_0^+ \times E \mapsto \mathbb{R}$ that satisfies Definition \ref{def:sol} and the limit condition $\lim_{x \to +\infty}q(t,x)=0$ locally uniformly with respect to $t \geq 0$.
<!-- end thm-4 -->
<!-- rmk-2: body_md -->
Notice that if $E \subset \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^d$ is a bounded open set with a suitably regular boundary, $f \in C_0(E)$ if and only if $f \in C(\overline{E})$ with $f(x)=0$ on $\partial E$. Furthermore,
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{x \to \infty}q(t,x)=0 \quad \mbox{ locally uniformly with respect to }t \ge 0
\end{equation*}
if and only if $q \in C(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times \overline{E})$ with $q(t,x)=0$ for all $x \in \partial E$. In particular (\ref{eq:main1}) becomes
    	\begin{equation}\label{eq:CD}
		\begin{cases}
		\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_{\phi}(t)q(0,x), &  t>0, \ x \in E,\\
		q(0,x)=u(x), & x \in E,\\
            q(t,x)=0 & x \in \partial E,
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation}
 i.e., it is a non-local Cauchy-Dirichlet problem. From a purely analytical point of view and in the special case $\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^\alpha$ and $G= \Delta$ these problems have been studied in \cite{ATHANASOPOULOS20182614} in connection with the obstacle problem for the fractional Laplacian and in \cite{fully1} with a non-linear term. Also in \cite{CaffarelliSilvestre+2014+63+83}, equations of these forms are considered and H\"older estimates are provided.
<!-- end rmk-2 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:AC}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:main1}, for all $x \in E$ the function $q(\cdot,x)$ belongs to ${\rm AC}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+)$ with a.e. derivative $\partial_t q(\cdot,x)$ satisfying, for almost all $t \in [0,T]$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:partialtcontrol}
	|\partial_t q(t,x)|\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})u^\phi(t)
\end{equation}
for a suitable constant $C>0$ (which is independent on $x \in E$). {Remember that $u^\phi (t)$ denotes the potential density}.
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Consider the Feller semigroup $(\widetilde{P}_t)_{t \ge 0}$ acting on any function $w \in C_0(E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$ as
	\begin{equation*}
		\widetilde{P}_tw(x,s)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[w(M(t),t+s)].
	\end{equation*}
	Let $\widetilde{A}$ be the generator of $(\widetilde{P}_t)_{t \ge 0}$. By Phillips theorem (see \cite[Theorem 12.6]{schilling2009bernstein}), we obtain that the generator of $(M^\phi,S)$ (which is obtained by subordinating $\widetilde{P}$ by means of the subordinator $S_0$) is given, on functions $w \in {\sf Dom}(\widetilde{A})$, by
	\begin{equation*}
		\widetilde{A}^\phi w=\int_0^{\infty}(\widetilde{P}_sw-w)\nu_\phi(ds)
	\end{equation*}
    {{where the integral is meant} in the sense of Bochner}.
	Now let $u \in {\sf Dom}(G)$ and consider, for any $N \in \operatorname{\mathbb{N}}$, a cut-off function of $[-N,N]$, i.e. $\eta_N \in C^\infty_c(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$ such that $\eta_N(t)=1$ for $t \in [-N,N]$, $\eta_N(t)=0$ for $t \in [-N-1,N+1]$ and $\eta_N(t) \in [0,1]$ for all $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$. Without loss of generality, we can assume that $\left\Vert \frac{d}{dt}\eta_N \right\Vert_{L^\infty(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})} \le C_\eta$, where $C_\eta$ is independent of $N$. We define $u_N(x,t)=u(x)\eta_N(t)$. Let us show that $u_N \in {\sf Dom}(\widetilde{A})$. Indeed,
	\begin{align*}
		&\lim_{h \to 0}\frac{\widetilde{P}_hu_N(x,t)-u_N(x,t)}{h}\notag \\ &=\lim_{h \to 0}\frac{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[u(M(h))\mid M(0)=x]\eta_N(t+h)-u(x)\eta_N(t)}{h}\\
		&=\lim_{h \to 0}\frac{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[u(M(h))\mid M(0)=x]-u(x)}{h}\eta_N(t+h)+u(x)\lim_{h \to 0}\frac{\eta_N(t+h)-\eta_N(t)}{h}\\
		&=Gu(x)\eta_N(t)+u(x)\frac{d}{dt}\eta_N(t).
	\end{align*}
	Since the pointwise generator coincides with the classical one (see \cite[Theorem 1.33]{bottcher2013levy}), then this proves that $u_N \in {\sf Dom}(\widetilde{A})$ and
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:ss}
		\widetilde{A}u_N(x,t)=Gu(x)\eta_N(t)+u(x)\frac{d}{dt}\eta_N(t) \ \forall x \in E, \ \forall t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}.
	\end{equation}
	By Phillips theorem, we know that $u_N \in {\sf Dom}(\widetilde{A}^\phi)$ and in particular
	\begin{align*}
		\widetilde{A}^\phi u_N&=\int_0^{\infty}(\widetilde{P}_su_N-u_N)\nu_\phi(ds)\\
		&=\int_0^{1}\int_0^s\widetilde{P}_\tau\widetilde{A} u_N d\tau \, \nu_\phi(ds)+\int_1^{\infty}(\widetilde{P}_su_N-u_N)\nu_\phi(ds).
	\end{align*}
	By Bochner theorem, see \cite[Theorem 1.1.4]{arendt2011vector}, we have
	\begin{align*}
		\left\Vert \widetilde{A}^\phi u_N \right\Vert_{C(E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})}& \le \int_0^{1}\int_0^s\left\Vert \widetilde{P}_\tau\widetilde{A} u_N \right\Vert_{C(E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})} d\tau \, \nu_\phi(ds)\notag \\
  &+\int_1^{\infty}\left\Vert \widetilde{P}_su_N-u_N \right\Vert_{C(E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})}\nu_\phi(ds)\\
		&\le \left\Vert \widetilde{A}u_N \right\Vert_{C(E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})}\mathcal{J}_\phi(1)+2\left\Vert u_N \right\Vert_{C(E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})}\overline{\nu}_\phi(1),
	\end{align*}
 {where $\mathcal{J}_\phi(1) = \int_0^1 s \nu_\phi (ds).$}
	Using (\ref{eq:ss})
                                          
        and recalling that $\left\Vert \eta_N \right\Vert_{L^\infty(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})}=1$, we have
	\begin{align*}
		\left\Vert \widetilde{A}^\phi u_N \right\Vert_{C(E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})}& \le (\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}C_\eta)\mathcal{J}_\phi(1)+2\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\overline{\nu}_\phi(1) \\
		&\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}),
	\end{align*}
	for some suitable constant $C$ that is independent of $N$. Now let
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:qNDynkin}
		q_N(t,x,s)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,s)}[u_N(X(t),S(L(t)-))]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,s)}[u_N(M^\phi(L(t)-),S(L(t)-))],
	\end{equation}
	observe that $L(t)$ is a stopping time with respect to the natural filtration of $(M^\phi(\cdot-),S(\cdot-))$ and use Dynkin's formula \cite[Equation (1.55)]{bottcher2013levy} to write
	\begin{equation*}
		q_N(t,x,s)=u_N(x,s)+\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,s)}\left[\int_0^{L(t)}\widetilde{A}^\phi u_N (M^\phi(\tau),S(\tau))d\tau\right],
	\end{equation*}
	where we also used the fact that {$S$} is stochastically continuous. Now fix $T>0$ and consider $t \in \left[0,\frac{3}{2}T\right]$. We extend $q_N(t,x,s)$ to $t \in \left[-\frac{T}{2},0\right]$ by setting $q_N(t,x,s)={u_N}(x,s)$. Let $h \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ with $0<|h|<\frac{T}{2}$ and define for any $\varphi:\left[-\frac{T}{2},\frac{3}{2}T\right] \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ the quantity
	\begin{equation*}
		D^h\varphi(t):=\frac{\varphi(t+h)-\varphi(t)}{h}.
	\end{equation*}
	We argue first for $h>0$. Observe that, by (\ref{eq:qNDynkin}), for almost all $t \in \left[0,T\right]$. 
	\begin{align*}
		\left|D^h q_N(t,x,0)\right| &\le h^{-1}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\int_{L(t)}^{L(t+h)}|\widetilde{A}^\phi u_N(M^\phi(\tau),S(\tau))|d\tau\right]\\
		&\le \left\Vert \widetilde{A}^\phi u_N \right\Vert_{C(E \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}})}h^{-1}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[L(t+h)-L(t)]\\
		&\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})h^{-1}(U^\phi(t+h)-U^\phi(t))\\
		&\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})u^\phi(t).
\end{align*}    
                                                                                                                                                                                
                                        
	                                                                         
	               
                      
                         
                                                                                        
                       
                                                                                                                                                                      
                                          
                                                                                                                                                     
                 
                                                                                                   
                   
                                                                                                            
                 
      
    If $h<0$ instead, let us distinguish among two cases. If $|h|\le\frac{t}{2}$ then we have
    \begin{equation*}
    \left|D^h q_N(t,x,0)\right|\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\frac{U^\phi(t)-U^\phi(t+h)}{|h|} \le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})u^\phi\left(\frac{t}{2}\right).
    \end{equation*}
    If, instead, $|h| \in \left[\frac{t}{2},t\right)$, then
    \begin{equation*}
    \left|D^h q_N(t,x,0)\right|\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\frac{U^\phi(t)-U^\phi(t+h)}{|h|} \le 2C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\frac{U^\phi\left(t\right)}{t}.
    \end{equation*}
    Finally, if $|h|>t$, we get
    \begin{equation*}
    \left|D^h q_N(t,x,0)\right|\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\frac{U^\phi(t)}{|h|} \le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\frac{U^\phi\left(t\right)}{t}.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, we have in general (for any $h \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$)
    \begin{equation*}
    \left|D^hq_N(t,x,0)\right| \le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\left(u^\phi(t)+u^\phi\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+\frac{U^\phi(t)}{t}\right).
    \end{equation*}
                                                                                                        
	               
	                                                                                                                                      
	                                                                              
	                                                           
	                                                  
	             
	Now, since $u \in C_0(E)$ and $\left\Vert \eta_N \right\Vert_{L^\infty(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})}=1$, we can use the dominated convergence theorem to take the limit as $N \to \infty$ and get
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:bound}
		\left|D^h q(t,x)\right| \le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\left(u^\phi(t)+u^\phi\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+\frac{U^\phi(t)}{t}\right).
	\end{equation}
	We now use a suitable modification of \cite[Theorem 5.8.3]{evans2010partial} in the case of $L^1$ functions. For completeness we give here all the details of the proof. Consider any sequence $h_n \to 0$, fix $x \in {E}$ and set, for simplicity, $g_n(t)=D^{h_n}q(t,x)$ for $t \in [0,T]$. Before proceeding, notice that for any $T>0$ it holds
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_0^T \frac{U^\phi(t)}{t}\, dt=\int_0^T \int_0^t \frac{u^\phi(s)}{t}\, ds \, dt=\int_0^T \int_s^T \frac{u^\phi(s)}{t}\, dt \, ds=\log(T)U^\phi(T)-\int_0^T\log(s)u^\phi(s) \, ds<\infty,
    \end{equation*}
    where the last inequality follows by assumption. Set then $g^\phi(t)=u^\phi(t)+u^\phi\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)+\frac{U^\phi(t)}{t}$ and notice that $g^\phi \in L^1_{\rm loc}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+)$ and $g_n(t) \le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})g^\phi(t)$ for all $t \ge 0$. We have then
    \begin{equation*}
	\left\Vert g_n \right\Vert_{L^1[0,T]} \le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\left\Vert g^\phi \right\Vert_{L^1[0,T]}, \ \forall n \in \operatorname{\mathbb{N}}.
    \end{equation*}
    Furthermore, since $g^\phi \in L^1[0,T]$, we know that for any $\varepsilon>0$ there exists $\delta>0$ such that for all measurable $A \subset [0,T]$ such that $|A|<\delta$ it holds
    \begin{equation*}
		\int_{A} g_n(t) dt  \le  C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\int_{A} g^\phi(t)\, dt<\varepsilon, \ \forall n \in \operatorname{\mathbb{N}},
\end{equation*}   
             
	                  
	                                                                                         
                
                                                                                                                                
                   
                                                                              
                 
                       
                      
	                                                       
	                
	that implies that the sequence $\{g_n\}_{n \in \operatorname{\mathbb{N}}}$ is uniformly integrable in $[0,T]$. Hence, by Dunford-Pettis theorem \cite[Theorem 4.30]{brezis2011functional} we know that there exists a function $q \in L^1(0,T)$ such that $g_n \upharpoonright g$. Next, let $\varphi \in C^\infty_c(0,T)$ and observe that
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^T D^hq(t,x)\varphi(t)dt=\int_0^{\frac{3}{2}T} D^hq(t,x)\varphi(t)dt=-\int_0^{\frac{3}{2}T} q(t,x)D^{-h}\varphi(t)dt
	\end{equation*}
	and, in particular,
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^T g_n(t)\varphi(t)dt=-\int_0^{\frac{3}{2}T} q(t,x)D^{-{h_n}}\varphi(t)dt.
	\end{equation*}
	Taking the limit as $n \to +\infty$ we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^T g(t)\varphi(t)dt=-\int_0^{\frac{3}{2}T} q(t,x)\varphi'(t)dt=-\int_0^{T} q(t,x)\varphi'(t)dt.
	\end{equation*}
	It follows that $q(\cdot,x) \in W^{1,1}[0,T]$, where $W^{1,1}[0,T]$ is the usual Sobolev space, with weak derivative $\partial_tq(t,x)=g(t)$. In particular, we recall that any function $W^{1,1}[0,T]$ admits an absolutely continuous version. Since $q(\cdot,x)$ is continuous, then it is absolutely continuous with a.e. derivative $\partial_t q(\cdot,x)$. Finally, notice that for $h>0$ we actually have
    \begin{equation*}
        \left|D^hq(t,x)\right| \le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})u^\phi(t)
    \end{equation*}
    and taking the limit as $h \to 0^+$ we get the statement.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- coro-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:ACest}
 Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main1} we have that for all $(t,x) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E$,
 \begin{equation*}
 \int_0^t \left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-P_sq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, \nu_\phi(ds)\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})
 \end{equation*}
 and
 \begin{equation*}
 \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t} \int_0^t \left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-P_sq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, \nu_\phi(ds) \, dt \le \frac{C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})}{\lambda}
 \end{equation*}
 for all $x \in E$ and $\lambda>0$.
<!-- end coro-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let us fix $x \in E$ and observe that by (\ref{eq:partialtcontrol}) we have
    \begin{align}\label{eq:partialcontrol2}
        \left|q(t-s,x)-q(t,x) \right| \, \leq \,& \int_0^s \left| \partial_tq(t-\tau,x) \right| d\tau \notag \\
        \leq \,& C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}) \int_0^s u^\phi(t-\tau)  d\tau.
    \end{align}
     
                                                                                  
                                            
                                                                                                                
                                                                                   
                
    Since $P_s$ is non-expansive, positive preserving and sub-Markov we have for all $x \in E$
    \begin{align}\label{eq:ACest1}
    \begin{split}
    |P_sq(t-s,x)-P_sq(t,x)|&\le P_s\left|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot) \right|(x) \\
    &\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}) \int_0^s u^\phi(t-\tau)  d\tau
    \end{split}
    \end{align}
    hence, taking the supremum, then integrating {and using Fubini},
    \begin{align*}
    \int_0^t &\left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-P_sq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, \nu_\phi(ds) \\
    &\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}) \int_0^t\int_0^s u^\phi(t-\tau) \, d\tau \, \nu_\phi(ds)\\
    &=C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\int_0^t u^\phi(t-\tau)  \int_\tau^t \nu_\phi(ds) \, {d\tau}\\
    &\le C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})\int_0^tu^\phi(t-\tau)\overline{\nu}_\phi(\tau)\, d\tau\\
    &=C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}),
    \end{align*}
 {where the first equality is due to the fact that $(\tau,s) \in [0,s] \times [0,t]$ is equivalent to $(\tau,s) \in [0,t] \times [\tau,t]$.}
    Furthermore, we have
    \begin{equation*}
    \int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_0^t \left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-P_sq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, \nu_\phi(ds)\, dt \le \frac{C(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)})}{\lambda}
    \end{equation*}
    for all $\lambda>0$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:regular2}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:main1}, for all $t \ge 0$ the function $q(t,\cdot)$ belongs to ${\sf Dom}(G)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:controlG}
	\left\Vert Gq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)} \le \left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}.
\end{equation}	
{Furthermore $\lim_{x \to \infty}q(t,x)=0$ locally uniformly with respect to $t \ge 0$.}
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Observe that, since $H(t)$ {defined in equation (\ref{eq:HH0Def})} is independent of $M$, we have
\begin{align}\label{eq:charq}
	q(t,x)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[u(X(t))]=&\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[u(X(t)) \mid H(t)]]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[P_{H(t)}u(x)] \notag \\ = \, &\int_0^t P_\tau u(x)\bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)d\tau,
\end{align}
where we used the fact that $H(t)$ admits density $\bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)$ for $\tau \in (0,t)$, see \cite[Lemma 1.10]{bertoin1999subordinators}. Let us stress that
\begin{equation*}
	\int_0^t \left\Vert P_\tau u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)d\tau \le \left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)},
\end{equation*}
hence, {a simple application of the dominated convergence theorem shows that $q(t,\cdot) \in C(E)$. Next, we prove that
\begin{equation}
\lim_{x \to \infty}q(t,x)=0 \quad \mbox{ locally uniformly with respect to }t \ge 0,
\end{equation}
which in turn implies that $q(t,\cdot) \in C_0(E)$. To do this, fix $T>0$ and observe that $P_tu \in C_0(E)$ for all $t \ge 0$ and $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ is strongly continuous. Let us extend the semigroup by setting $P_t=I$ for all $t \le 0$, in such a way that $(P_t)_{t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}}$ is still a strongly continuous family of operators such that $P_tu \in C_0(E)$ for all $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$. Fix $\varepsilon>0$. For all $t \in [0,T]$ let $K(t)$ be a compact set such that $\sup_{x \not \in K(t)}|P_tu(x)|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$. Since $P_{t+h}u$ uniformly converges towards $P_tu$ as $h \to 0$, we know that there exists $\delta(t)>0$ such that for any $s \in (t-\delta(t),t+\delta(t))=:I(t)$ it holds $\sup_{x \not \in K(t)}|P_{s}u(x)-P_tu(x)|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$. As a consequence, in particular, $\sup_{x \not \in K(t)}|P_su(x)|<\varepsilon$. Now notice that $\bigcup_{t \in [0,T]}I(t) \supset [0,T]$, where the latter is a compact subset of $\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$, thus $\{I(t)\}_{t \in [0,T]}$ is an open covering of $[0,T]$. Hence, we can extract a finite number of such open sets $I(t_1),\dots,I(t_N)$ such that $[0,T] \subset \bigcup_{i=1}^{N}I(t_i)$. Let $K=\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}K(t_i)$, which is a compact set since it is a finite union of compact sets. Let $s \in [0,T]$. Then there exists $i=1,\dots,N$ such that $s \in I(t_i)$ and then
\begin{equation*}
\sup_{x \not \in K}|P_su(x)| \le \sup_{x \not \in K(t_i)}|P_su(x)|<\varepsilon.
\end{equation*}
We have shown that, for all $s \in [0,T]$,
\begin{equation*}
\sup_{x \not \in K}|P_su(x)|<\varepsilon.
\end{equation*}
Hence, by (\ref{eq:altrapq}) we get
\begin{equation*}
\sup_{x \not \in K}|q(t,x)| \le \int_0^t \overline{\nu}_\phi(t-s)u^\phi(s)\sup_{x \not \in K}|P_su(x)|\, ds<\varepsilon\int_0^t \overline{\nu}_\phi(t-s)u^\phi(s)\, ds=\varepsilon. 
\end{equation*}
Since $\varepsilon>0$ and $T>0$ are arbitrary, we proved that $\lim_{x \to \infty}q(t,x)=0$ locally uniformly with respect to $t \ge 0$. Now we need to prove that $q(t,\cdot) \in {\sf Dom}(G)$ and that (\ref{eq:controlG}) holds.} Define
\begin{equation*}
	G_s=\frac{P_s-\mathds{I}}{s}, \ s>0,
\end{equation*}
where $\mathds{I}$ is the identity operator on $C_0(E)$.  Setting $Q:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \to C_0(E)$ as $Q(t)=q(t,\cdot)$ for $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+$, we have
\begin{equation*}
	Q(t)=\int_0^t P_\tau u\bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)d\tau.
\end{equation*}
Thus,
\begin{align}\label{eq:Gstildeq}
	G_sQ(t)=G_s \int_0^t P_\tau u\, \bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)d\tau= \, &\int_0^t G_sP_\tau u \, \bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)d\tau\notag \\ = \, &\int_0^t P_\tau G_s u \, \bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)d\tau,
\end{align}
where we used \cite[Proposition 1.1.6]{arendt2011vector} and the fact that $G_s$ and $P_\tau$ commute on ${\sf Dom}(G)$. Now, since $u \in {\sf Dom}(G)$, we have {(see definition \ref{def:semigroupaction})}
\begin{equation*}
	G_su=\frac{1}{s}\int_0^sP_wGudw
\end{equation*}
and in particular $\left\Vert G_su \right\Vert_{C(E)} \le \left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}$. Furthermore, since $P_\tau$ is contractive, we have
\begin{equation*}
	\left\Vert P_\tau G_s u \, \bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau) \right\Vert_{C(E)} \le \bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)},
\end{equation*}
where the right-hand side is integrable in $[0,t]$ and independent of $s$. Hence, taking the limit as $s \to 0$ in (\ref{eq:Gstildeq}), using the dominated convergence theorem for Bochner integrals \cite[Theorem 1.1.8]{arendt2011vector} and the fact that $P_\tau$ is continuous, we get
\begin{equation*}
	\lim_{s \to 0}G_sQ(t)=\int_0^t P_\tau G u \, \bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)d\tau,
\end{equation*}
i.e. $q(t,\cdot) \in {\sf Dom}(G)$ for all $t \ge 0$ and
\begin{equation*}
	Gq(t,\cdot)=\int_0^t P_\tau G u(\cdot) \, \bar{\nu}_\phi(t-\tau)u^\phi(\tau)d\tau.
\end{equation*}
Finally, again by Bochner theorem, the contractivity of $P_\tau$ and the fact that $(\bar{\nu}_\phi,u^\phi)$ is a Sonine pair, we have
\begin{equation*}
	\left\Vert Gq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)} \le \left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- coro-2: body_md -->
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main1} we have, for all $(t,x) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times E$
 \begin{equation*}
     \int_0^t \left\Vert P_sq(t,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, \nu_\phi(ds) \le \left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}\mathcal{J}_\phi(t)
 \end{equation*}
 {where $\mathcal{J}_\phi(t) = \int_0^t s \nu_\phi (d s)$} and, for all $\lambda>0$ and $x \in E$,
 \begin{equation*}
     \int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\int_0^t \left\Vert P_sq(t,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, \nu_\phi(ds)\, dt<\infty.
 \end{equation*}
<!-- end coro-2 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Since $q(t,\cdot) \in {\sf Dom}(G) \subset C_0(E)$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)= \int_0^s GP_\tau q(t,x)\,d\tau= \int_0^s P_\tau G q(t,x)\, d\tau.
\end{equation*}
Taking the absolute value, using again the fact that $P_\tau$ is non-expansive, positivity preserving and sub-Markov,
\begin{multline}\label{eq:Gest1}
    |P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)| \le \int_0^s |P_\tau G q(t,x)|\, d\tau \le \int_0^s P_\tau |G q(t,x)|\, d\tau 
    \le \left\Vert Gq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}s \le \left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}s.  
\end{multline}
Hence, taking the supremum and then integrating,
 \begin{equation*}
     \int_0^t \left\Vert P_sq(t,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, \nu_\phi(ds) \le \left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}\mathcal{J}_\phi(t)
 \end{equation*}
 and it follows by (\ref{Jphi}), that $\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\mathcal{J}_\phi(t)\, dt<\infty$ for all $\lambda>0$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{prop55}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:main1} {the following inequality} holds, for all $\lambda>0$ and $x \in E$
\begin{equation*}
    \int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)q(t,x)dt \le \frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
First, notice that, by the discussion in Subsection \ref{secregq}, we know that we are under the hypotheses of Theorems \ref{thm:integrability2} and \ref{thm:Lapinside2}. Hence, observe that by Theorem~\ref{thm:integrability2}, $\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)$ is well-defined for all $x \in E$ and $t>0$. Furthermore, by Theorem~\ref{thm:Lapinside2}, we know that for all $x \in E$ the function $\phi(\partial_t-G)q(\cdot,x)$ is Laplace transformable for any $\lambda>0$. 
                                                                                                                                      
		{Consider that} $q(0,x)=u(x)$ by definition. Next, observe that, for $\lambda>0$, {the Laplace transform of the right-hand side of the first equation in (\ref{eq:main1}) is given by}
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)q(0,x)dt=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}q(0,x),
	\end{equation*}
    {where $q(0,x)$ does not play any role in the determination of the aforementioned Laplace transform. Hence, we need to prove that for all $x \in E$}
                                          
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}q(0,x){=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)q(0,x)dt},
	\end{equation*}
    {so that the injectivity of the Laplace transform then yields the first equation for fixed $x \in E$ and almost all $t>0$.} 
For this, first observe that by Theorem~\ref{thm:Lapinside2} we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt=-\int_0^{\infty}\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}(P_sq(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)-q(t,x))dt\, \nu_\phi(ds).
	\end{equation*}
                                             
	Set $Q$ as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:regular2}. Then we have
	\begin{align*}
		\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}P_sQ(t-s)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)dt&=P_s\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}Q(t-s)\mathds{1}_{[s,+\infty)}(t)dt\\
		&=P_s\int_s^\infty e^{-\lambda t}Q(t-s)dt\\
		&=e^{-\lambda s}P_s\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}Q(t)dt:=e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{Q}(\lambda),
	\end{align*}
where {the first equality is due to the fact that $P_s$ does not depend on $t$ and that $s \in [0,t]$ is equivalent to $t \in [s,\infty)$ and} where all the integrals are Bochner integrals on $C_0(E)$. Hence, in particular, the equality holds for all $x \in E$ and then we get
\begin{align*}
	\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}P_sq(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s)dt=e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x),
\end{align*}
where $\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}q(t,x)dt$. This implies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:preLaplace}
	\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt=-\int_0^{\infty} \left(e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)-\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\right) \nu_\phi(ds).
\end{equation}
Now we need to evaluate $\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)$. First, we use Fubini's theorem to write
\begin{equation*}
	\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}u(X(t))\, dt\right].
\end{equation*}
Then, we recall that \textcolor{blue}{$S$} performs at most countably many jumps that are a.s. dense in $[0, +\infty)$ and it increases continuously on a set with Lebesgue measure zero (see \cite[Chapter 4, Section 21]{ken1999levy}) and also that $H(t)=S(y-)$ for any $t \in (S(y-),S(y)]$, so that                                                                                                              
\begin{align*}
	\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y \ge 0}\int_{S(y-)}^{S(y)}e^{-\lambda t}u(M(S(y-)))dt\right]\\
	&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y \ge 0}\frac{e^{-\lambda S(y-)}-e^{-\lambda S(y)}}{\lambda}u(M(S(y-)))\right]\\
                                                                                                                                        
	&=\frac{1}{\lambda}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y \ge 0}e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda (S(y)-S(y-))})u(M(S(y-)))\right]\\
                                                                                                                                                     
\end{align*}
Now observe that all the previous argument can be applied to $u_{\pm}(x)= \max\{0,\pm u(x)\}$, so that
\begin{align}\label{eq:presubtract}
&\frac{1}{\lambda}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y \ge 0}e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda (S(y)-S(y-))})u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))\right] \notag \\
                                                                                                                                                                   
=	\, &\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^x[u_{\pm}(X(t))]dt<\infty.
\end{align}
Consider the $C_{\rm b}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+)$-valued stochastic processes: $Z_{\pm}:=\{Z_{\pm}(y), y \ge 0\}$ defined, for all $y,z \ge 0$, as
                                                                                                                                                                 
\begin{equation*}
	Z_{\pm}(y)(z)=u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda z}).
\end{equation*}
                  
                                                                     
                
Notice that $Z_{\pm}$ is left-continuous, i.e. for all $y>0$ and $y_n \uparrow y$ it holds
\begin{equation*}
\left\Vert Z_{\pm}(y_n)-Z_{\pm}(y) \right\Vert_{C(E)}
\le |u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}-u_{\pm}(M(S(y_n-)))e^{-\lambda S(y_n-)}| \to 0
\end{equation*}
as $n \to \infty$, hence it is a predictable process
                                                             
Furthermore, observe that $\mathfrak{p}=\{\mathfrak{p}(y), \ y \ge 0\}$ with $\mathfrak{p}(y)=S(y)-S(y-)$ is a Poisson point process of intensity $\nu_\phi$. Hence, by the compensation formula (see \cite[Page $7$]{bertoin1996levy}), we get
\begin{align*}
	\frac{1}{\lambda}&\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y \ge 0}e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda (S(y)-S(y-))})u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))\right]\\
                                                                                                                                                                                
	&=\frac{1}{\lambda}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\int_0^{+\infty}\left(\int_0^{+\infty}(1-e^{-\lambda w})\nu_\phi(dw)\right)u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}dy\right]\\
	&=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\int_0^{+\infty}u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}dy\right]\\
	&=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{+\infty}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u_{\pm}(M(S(y-)))e^{-\lambda S(y-)}\right]dy \notag \\ =& \frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{+\infty}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u_{\pm}(M(S(y)))e^{-\lambda S(y)}\right]dy,
\end{align*}
where in the last equality we used the fact that $S$ is stochastically continuous and independent of $M$. Since the left-hand side of the previous equality is finite, so it is the right-hand side. Hence, subtracting term to term and {using the fact that $u(x)=u_+(x)-u_-(x)$ according to the definition of $u_{\pm}$}, we get                                                                                        
\begin{align*}
	\widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\frac{1}{\lambda}&\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\sum_{y \ge 0}e^{-\lambda S(y-)}(1-e^{-\lambda (S(y)-S(y-))})u(M(S(y-))) \right]\\
	&=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{+\infty}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(S(y)))e^{-\lambda S(y)}\right]dy\\
	&=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda \tau}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy,
\end{align*}
where $g_S(\tau;y)d\tau$ is the law of $S(y)$ under $\mathds{P}^{(x,0)}$ and we used the independence of $M$ and $S$. Substituting this equality into (\ref{eq:preLaplace}) we get
\begin{align}
	\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}&\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt \nonumber \\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \left[e^{-\lambda s}P_s\left(\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda \tau}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy\right)(x)\right.\nonumber\\
	&-\left.\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda \tau}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy\right] \nu_\phi(ds). \nonumber
\end{align}
Now observe that
\begin{align*}
	\int_0^{+\infty}& \int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda \tau}\left\Vert \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}[u(M(\tau))] \right\Vert_{C(E)}g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy\\
	&\le \left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda \tau}g_S(\tau; y)d\tau\, dy \\
	&=\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-y\phi(\lambda)}dy=\frac{\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}}{\phi(\lambda)}<\infty,
\end{align*}
hence, by \cite[Proposition 1.1.6]{arendt2011vector} and recalling that Bochner integrability in $C_0(E)$ implies Lebesgue integrability for fixed $x \in E$, we have
\begin{align}
	\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}&\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt \nonumber \\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda (s+\tau)}P_s\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)\right.\nonumber\\
	&\qquad -\left.e^{-\lambda \tau}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right) g_S(\tau;y)d\tau \, dy \nu_\phi(ds).\label{eq:preLaplace2}
\end{align}
                                            
Next, we want to use Fubini theorem. To do this, we first observe that $\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}[u(M(\tau))] \in {\sf Dom}(G)$. Furthermore, the semigroup $\{\widetilde{P}_s\}_{s \ge 0}$ where $\widetilde{P}_s=e^{-\lambda s}P_s$ admits $G-\lambda I$ as generator, with domain ${\sf Dom}(G)$. Hence,
\begin{align*}
	\int_0^{\infty} &\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\left|e^{-\lambda (s+\tau)}P_s\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)-
	          
	                
	e^{-\lambda \tau}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right| g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy \nu_\phi(ds)\\
	&=\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda \tau}\left|\int_0^{s}\widetilde{P}_w (G-\lambda I)\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)dw\right| g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy \nu_\phi(ds)\\
	&\le \int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^s e^{-\lambda \tau} \left| \widetilde{P}_w (G-\lambda I)\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)\right| dw\, g_S(\tau;y)d\tau \, dy \, \nu_\phi(ds)\\
	&\le \int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^s e^{-\lambda \tau}\left\Vert \widetilde{P}_w (G-\lambda I)\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right] \right\Vert_{C(E)}dw\, g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy\, \nu_\phi(ds)\\
	&\le \int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-\lambda \tau}\left\Vert (G-\lambda I)\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right] \right\Vert_{C(E)}\left(\int_0^{s}e^{-\lambda w}dw\right) g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy\, \nu_\phi(ds)\\
	&\le \frac{\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\lambda \left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}}{\lambda}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty} {\int_0^{+\infty}}e^{-\lambda \tau}(1-e^{-\lambda s}) g_S(\tau;y)d\tau\, dy\, \nu_\phi(ds)\\
	&=\frac{\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\lambda \left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}}{\lambda}<\infty.
\end{align*}
Hence, by Fubini theorem, changing the order of the integrals in (\ref{eq:preLaplace2}), we have
\begin{align}
	\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}&\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt \nonumber \\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda (s+\tau)}P_s\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)\right.\nonumber\\
	&\qquad -\left.e^{-\lambda \tau}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right) \left(\int_0^{+\infty} g_S(\tau;y)dy\right) \, d\tau \nu_\phi(ds)\nonumber\\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\left(e^{-\lambda (s+\tau)}P_s\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)\right.\nonumber\\
	&\qquad
	 -\left.e^{-\lambda \tau}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right]\right)u^\phi(\tau) \, d\tau \nu_\phi(ds),\nonumber
	 \end{align}
where we used the relation $u^\phi(\tau)=\int_0^{+\infty}g_S(\tau;y)dy$. Again, since $\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}[u(M(\tau))] \in {\sf Dom}(G)$, we can write
\begin{align}
	\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}&\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt \nonumber \\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^s e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}(G-\lambda)P_w\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)u^\phi(\tau) dw \, d\tau \nu_\phi(ds).\nonumber
\end{align}
Next, we use again Fubini theorem and we get{, by observing that $s > w$,}
\begin{align}
	\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}&\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt \nonumber \\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty}\int_w^{\infty} e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}(G-\lambda)P_w\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)u^\phi(\tau) \nu_\phi(ds) \, dw \, d\tau\nonumber\\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}(G-\lambda)P_w\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}\left[u(M(\tau))\right](x)u^\phi(\tau) \overline{\nu}_\phi(w) \, dw \, d\tau\nonumber\\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}(G-\lambda)P_{w+\tau}u(x)\, u^\phi(\tau) \overline{\nu}_\phi(w) \, dw \, d\tau.\label{eq:preLaplace3}
\end{align}
Now we set $w+\tau=v$ in the inner integral so that (\ref{eq:preLaplace3}) gives us {(again, remember that $\tau \in [0,v]$)}
\begin{align}
	\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}&\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt \nonumber \\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty} \int_\tau^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda v}(G-\lambda)P_{v}u(x)\, u^\phi(\tau) \overline{\nu}_\phi(v-\tau) \, dv \, d\tau\nonumber \\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty}  e^{-\lambda v}(G-\lambda)P_{v}u(x)\, \left(\int_0^v u^\phi(\tau) \overline{\nu}_\phi(v-\tau) \, d\tau\right) \, dv\nonumber \\
	&=-\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty}  e^{-\lambda v}(G-\lambda)P_{v}u(x) dv\nonumber \\
	&=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}\int_0^{\infty}  e^{-\lambda v}P_{v}(\lambda-G)u(x) dv\nonumber\\ 
	&=\frac{\phi(\lambda)}{\lambda}u(x),
\end{align}
where we used the fact that $u^\phi$ and $\overline{\nu}_\phi$ constitute a Sonine pair {as a consequence of (\ref{eq:densityH})}, $P_v$ and $G$ commute on ${\sf Dom}(G)$ and 
\begin{equation*}
\int_0^{\infty}  e^{-\lambda v}P_{v} dv=(\lambda I-G)^{-1}.
\end{equation*}
Taking the inverse Laplace transform we get that, for all $x \in E$, there exists $I_x \subset \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+$, with Lebesgue measure zero, such that for $t \not \in I_x$ it holds
\begin{equation*}
\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)u(x).
\end{equation*}
Now we need to show that $I_x={\varnothing}$ for all $x \in E$. To this purpose, first denote by $\mathcal{R}=\overline{\{{S}(t), \ t \ge 0\}}$ the topological closure of the range of \textcolor{blue}{$S$}. Since $H$ can only jump when $t \in \mathcal{R}$ and it is isolated on the left,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:contH}
    \mathds{P}^{(x,0)}(H(t)\not = H(t+)) \le \mathds{P}^{(x,0)}(t \in \mathcal{R})=0
\end{equation}
by \cite[Proposition 1.9]{bertoin1999subordinators}, since $\phi \in \mathfrak{SB}_0$. Once this has been established, notice that one can rewrite, given that $H$ and $M$ are independent, 
\begin{multline*}
q(t,x)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[u(M(H(t))]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[u(M(H(t)) \mid H(t)\right]\right]\\
=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[P_{H(t)}u(x)\right]=\int_0^t P_su(x)\overline{\nu}_\phi(t-s)u^\phi(s)\,ds.
\end{multline*}
Notice, however, that for any two fixed $x_0,x \in E$ it holds
\begin{equation}\label{eq:altrapq}
    q(t,x)=\int_0^t P_su(x)\overline{\nu}_\phi(t-s)u^\phi(s)\,ds=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x_0,0)}\left[P_{H(t)}u(x)\right].
\end{equation}
Fix $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+$ and let $t_n \to t$. Then it holds
\begin{equation*}
\left\Vert q(t_n,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)} \le \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x_0,0)}\left[\left\Vert P_{H(t_n)}u-P_{H(t)}u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\right].
\end{equation*}
Notice that $\lim_{n \to \infty}\left\Vert P_{H(t_n)}u-P_{H(t)}u \right\Vert_{C(E)}=0$ almost surely by (\ref{eq:contH}) and the strong continuity of $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$. Furthermore, $\left\Vert P_{H(t_n)}u-P_{H(t)}u \right\Vert_{C(E)} \le 2\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}$. Hence, by the dominated convergence theorem,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniformcont}
\lim_{n \to \infty}\left\Vert q(t_n,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}=0.
\end{equation}
In particular, this shows that $q$ is continuous in both variables. Next, fix $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+$ and consider a sequence $t_n \downarrow t$. Then we have for all $x \in E$
    \begin{align*}
        |\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t_n,x)&-\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)|\\
        & \le \int_0^{t}|P_sq(t-s,x)-q(t,x)-P_sq(t_n-s,x))+q(t_n,x)|\nu_\phi(ds)\\
        &\qquad +\int_{t}^{t_n}|P_sq(t_n-s,x)-q(t_n,x)|\nu_\phi(ds)\\
        &\qquad +|q(t,x)|(\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n))+|q(t,x)-q(t_n,x)|\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n).
    \end{align*}
Taking the supremum over $x \in E$, this leads to
    \begin{align*}
        &\left\Vert \phi(\partial_t-G)q(t_n,\cdot)-\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\\
        &\le \int_0^{t}\left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)-P_sq(t_n-s,\cdot))+q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\nu_\phi(ds)\\
        &\qquad +\int_{t}^{t_n}\left\Vert P_sq(t_n-s,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\nu_\phi(ds)\\
        &\qquad +\left\Vert q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}(\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n))+\left\Vert q(t,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n)\\
        &:=I_n+J_n+\left\Vert q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}(\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n))+\left\Vert q(t,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n).
    \end{align*}
Concerning $I_n$, first notice that
\begin{align*}
&\left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)-P_sq(t_n-s,\cdot))+q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)} \\
&\quad \le \left\Vert P_s(q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t_n-s,\cdot)\textcolor{blue}{)} \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert q(t,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\\
&\quad \le \left\Vert q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t_n-s,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert q(t,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)},
\end{align*}
since $P_s$ is non-expansive. Hence, taking the limit as $n \to \infty$ and using (\ref{eq:uniformcont}) we have
\begin{align*}
\lim_{n \to \infty}\left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)-P_sq(t_n-s,\cdot))+q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}=0.
\end{align*}
Furthermore,
\begin{align*}
&\left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)-P_sq(t_n-s,\cdot)+q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)} \\
&\quad \le \left\Vert P_sq(t-s,\cdot)-P_sq(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert P_sq(t,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\\
&+\left\Vert P_sq(t_n-s,\cdot)-P_sq(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert P_sq(t_n,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\\
&\le 2C\left(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\right)\int_0^su^\phi(t-\tau)d\tau+2\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}s,
\end{align*}
where we used (\ref{eq:ACest1}), (\ref{eq:Gest1}) and the fact that $u^\phi(t_n-\tau) \le u^\phi(t-\tau)$ since $u^\phi$ is non-increasing. The right-hand side of the previous inequality is integrable against $\nu_\phi(ds)$, hence we can use the dominated convergence theorem to achieve
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{n \to \infty}I_n=0.
\end{equation*}
Concerning $J_n$ instead, we have
\begin{align*}
    &\left\Vert P_sq(t_n-s,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)} \\
    &\quad \le \left\Vert P_sq(t_n-s,\cdot)-P_s q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert P_sq(t_n,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}\\
    &\quad \le C\left(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\right)\int_0^su^\phi(t^n-\tau)d\tau+\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}s,
\end{align*}
where we used again (\ref{eq:ACest1}) and (\ref{eq:Gest1}). Hence
\begin{align*}
    J_n &\le C\left(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\right)\int_{t}^{t_n}\int_0^su^\phi(t^n-\tau)d\tau\nu_\phi(ds)+\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}\int_t^{t_n}s\nu_\phi(ds)\\
    &=C\left(\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}+\left\Vert u \right\Vert_{C(E)}\right)J_n^1+\left\Vert Gu \right\Vert_{C(E)}(\mathcal{J}_\phi(t_n)-\mathcal{J}_\phi(t)).
\end{align*}
By (\ref{Jphi}), it turns out that $\mathcal{J}_\phi$ is right-continuous. Furthermore
\begin{align*}
    J_n^1&=\int_{t}^{t_n}\int_0^tu^\phi(t^n-\tau)d\tau\nu_\phi(ds)+\int_{t}^{t_n}\int_t^su^\phi(t^n-\tau)d\tau\nu_\phi(ds)\\
    &=\left(\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n)\right)(U^\phi(t_n)-U^\phi(t_n-t))\\
    &\qquad+\int_{t}^{t_n}u^\phi(t_n-\tau)(\overline{\nu}_\phi(\tau)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n))\, d\tau\\
    &=\left(\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n)\right)(U^\phi(t_n)-U^\phi(t_n-t))\\
    &\qquad +\int_{t}^{t_n}u^\phi(t_n-\tau)\overline{\nu}_\phi(\tau)\, d\tau-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n)U^\phi(t_n-t)\\
    &\le \left(\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n)\right)(U^\phi(t_n)-U^\phi(t_n-t))\\
    &\qquad +\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)U^\phi(t_n-t)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n)U^\phi(t_n-t)\\
    &=\left(\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)-\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n)\right)U^\phi(t_n),
\end{align*}
hence, since $\overline{\nu}_\phi$ is also right-continuous, $\lim_{n \to \infty}J_n^1=0$, thus $\lim_{n \to \infty}J_n=0$. This proves that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rightcontG}
    \lim_{n \to \infty}\left\Vert \phi(\partial_t-G)q(t_n,\cdot)-\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}=0.
\end{equation}
Now fix $x \in E$ and assume, by contradiction, that $I_x \not = {\varnothing}$. Let $t \in I_x$ and consider any interval of the form $[t,t+\delta]$ for some $\delta>0$. Let $I_x^\delta=I_x \cap [t,t+\delta]$ and observe that $|I_x^\delta|=0$, hence $|[t,t+\delta]\setminus I_x^\delta|=\delta=|[t,t+\delta]|$. Since the Lebesgue measure has full topological support (i.e., any non-empty open set has positive measure), then $[t,t+\delta]\setminus I_x^\delta$ is dense in $[t,t+\delta]$ and there exists a sequence $t_n \downarrow t$ such that $t_n \not \in I_x$ for all $n \in \operatorname{\mathbb{N}}$. For such a sequence
\begin{equation*}
\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t_n,x)=\overline{\nu}_\phi(t_n)q(t_n,x).
\end{equation*}
However, taking the limit as $n \to \infty$, using (\ref{eq:uniformcont}), (\ref{eq:rightcontG}) and the fact that $\overline{\nu}_\phi$ is right-continuous, we get
\begin{equation*}
\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)q(t,x),
\end{equation*}
that is absurd since $t \in I_x$. Hence $I_x = {\varnothing}$ and, since $x \in E$ is arbitrary, this shows that
\begin{equation}
\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)q(t,x), \quad \forall (t,x) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times E.
\end{equation}
\begin{comment}

\end{comment}
{Combining the latter with the fact that $\lim\limits_{x \to \infty}q(t,x)=0$ locally uniformly with respect to $t \ge 0$ as shown in Proposition \ref{prop:regular2},} we finally have that $q$ is solution of (\ref{eq:main1}).

It remains to show that this is the unique solution. Indeed, let $q_1$ be another solution of (\ref{eq:main1}). Then $w=q-q_1$ solves (\ref{eq:main1}) with $u \equiv 0$. Now let $T>0$ and assume, by contradiction, that $\varepsilon=\sup_{(t,x) \in [0,T] \times E}|w(t,x)|>0$. Let $K \subset E$ be such that
\begin{equation*}
    \sup_{x \not \in K}|w(t,x)|<\varepsilon, \ \forall t \in [0,T].
\end{equation*}
Then, since $w$ is continuous,
\begin{equation*}
    \varepsilon=\max_{(t,x) \in [0,T]\times K}|w(t,x)|.
\end{equation*}
Let $(t_\star,x_\star) \in [0,T]\times K$ be such that $|w(t_\star,x_\star)|=\varepsilon$. If $w(t_\star,x_\star)=\varepsilon$, then $w(t_\star,x_\star) \ge w(t,x)$ for all $(t,x) \in (0,t_\star] \times E$ and $w(t_\star,x_\star)=\varepsilon>0=w(0,x)$ for all $x \in E$. Hence, by Proposition \ref{prop:maxprin} we have
\begin{equation*}
    0=-\phi(\partial_t-G)w(t_\star,x_\star)<0,
\end{equation*}
which is a contradiction. The same holds if $w(t_\star,x_\star)=-\varepsilon$. Hence $w \equiv 0$ and this ends the proof.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:unique2}
    Let $q:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ be a function such that $q(t,\cdot) \in {\sf Dom}(G)$ for all $t \ge 0$ and $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \mapsto q(t,\cdot) \in C_0(E)$ is measurable. Assume further that there exists $\lambda_\star>0$ such that:
    \begin{itemize}
    \item The function
    \begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}q(t,x)dt
    \end{equation*}
    is well-defined for all $x \in E$ and $\lambda>\lambda_\star$
    \item It holds
    \begin{equation*}
        \left\Vert \widetilde{q} \right\Vert_{C(E)} \le \int_0^{\infty}e^{-\lambda_\star t}\left\Vert q(t,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}dt<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
    \item For all $x \in E$ there exists $\mathcal{I}_{x} \subset \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+$ such that $|\mathcal{I}_x|=0$ and $\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)$ is well-defined for all $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \setminus \mathcal{I}_x$. 
    \item For all $x \in E$ and $\lambda>\lambda_\star$ it holds
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:exchange}
        \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}\phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)dt=-\int_0^\infty\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}(P_sq(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)-q(t,x))dt\, \nu_\phi(ds)
    \end{equation}
    \item For all $x \in E$ and $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+\setminus \mathcal{I}_x$ it holds
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:eqspecial}
    \phi(\partial_t-G)q(t,x)=\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)q(0,x), \quad \mbox{ for almost all }t>0.
    \end{equation}
    \end{itemize}
    Then, for almost all $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+$ it holds
    \begin{equation*}
        \left\Vert q(t,\cdot)-\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(\cdot,0)}[q(0,X(t))] \right\Vert_{C(E)}=0.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
