Since $q(0,\cdot) \in {\sf Dom(G)}$, arguing as in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:main1}, we know that the function $q_\star(t,x)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}[q(0,X(t))]$ satisfies all the assumptions in the statement. As a consequence, also the function $q-q_\star$ satisfies all the assumptions in the statement. Hence, without loss of generality, we can assume that $q(0,\cdot) \equiv 0$. Integrating (\ref{eq:eqspecial}) against $e^{-\lambda t}$ for $\lambda>\lambda_\star$ and using (\ref{eq:exchange}) we get
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:equality0}
        \int_0^\infty\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}(P_sq(t-s,x_\star)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)-q(t,x))dt\, \nu_\phi(ds)=0.
    \end{equation}
    Next, notice that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Pbeforeex}
        \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}P_sq(t-s,x) \mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)dt=e^{-\lambda s}\int_0^\infty P_s\left(e^{-\lambda (t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot) \mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)\right)(x)dt.
    \end{equation}
    Now observe that
    \begin{align}\label{eq:Bochnerint}
    \begin{split}
        \int_0^\infty \left\Vert e^{-\lambda (t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot) \mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s) \right\Vert_{C(E)}dt&=\int_0^\infty \left\Vert e^{-\lambda (t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot) \mathds{1}_{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+}(t-s) \right\Vert_{C(E)}dt\\
        &=\int_{-s}^\infty \left\Vert e^{-\lambda \tau}q(\tau,\cdot) \mathds{1}_{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+}(\tau) \right\Vert_{C(E)}d\tau\\
        &=\int_{0}^\infty e^{-\lambda \tau}\left\Vert q(\tau,\cdot) \right\Vert_{C(E)}d\tau<\infty.
        \end{split}
    \end{align}
    Observing that, for fixed $s>0$, also $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \mapsto e^{-\lambda (t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot) \mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s) \in C_0(E)$ is measurable, we know by (\ref{eq:Bochnerint}) and Bochner's theorem (see \cite[Theorem 1.1.4]{arendt2011vector}) that the latter is Bochner-integrable and then, by \cite[Proposition 1.1.6]{arendt2011vector} and (\ref{eq:Pbeforeex}), we have
    \begin{multline}
        \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}P_sq(t-s,x) \mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)dt=e^{-\lambda s}P_s\left(\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda (t-s)}q(t-s,\cdot) \mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)(x)dt\right)=e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{q}(x)
    \end{multline}
    and then (\ref{eq:equality0}) becomes
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:equality1}
        \int_0^\infty \left(e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{q}(x)-\widetilde{q}(x)\right) \nu_\phi(ds)=0.
    \end{equation}
    Now let $\widetilde{P}_s=e^{-\lambda s}P_s$ and notice that this is still a Feller semigroup with generator $G-\lambda I$, where $I$ is the identity operator. In particular, by \cite[Proposition 1.1.7]{arendt2011vector}, $\widetilde{q} \in {\sf Dom}(G) \subset {\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)$. In particular, this implies that
    \begin{equation*}
        e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{q}(x)-\widetilde{q}(x)=\int_0^s \widetilde{P}_\tau(G-\lambda I)\widetilde{q}(x)\, d\tau.
    \end{equation*}
    Taking the norm we get
    \begin{align*}
        \left\Vert e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{q}-\widetilde{q} \right\Vert_{C(E)} &\le \int_0^s \left\Vert \widetilde{P}_\tau(G-\lambda I)\widetilde{q} \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, d\tau\le \frac{\left\Vert (G-\lambda I)\widetilde{q} \right\Vert_{C(E)}}{\lambda}(1-e^{-\lambda s}).
    \end{align*}
    Integrating the right-hand side against $\nu_\phi(ds)$ we get
    \begin{align*}
        \int_0^\infty \left\Vert e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{q}-\widetilde{q} \right\Vert_{C(E)}\, \nu_\phi(ds) \le \frac{\left\Vert (G-\lambda I)\widetilde{q} \right\Vert_{C(E)}}{\lambda}\phi(\lambda).
    \end{align*}
    Thus, we can rewrite (\ref{eq:equality1}) in terms of Bochner integrals as
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:equality2}
        \int_0^\infty \left(e^{-\lambda s}P_s\widetilde{q}-\widetilde{q}\right) \nu_\phi(ds)=0.
    \end{equation}
    Now consider the Feller semigroup $(P_s^\phi)_{s \ge 0}$ acting on $f \in C_0(E)$ as
    \begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{P}_s^\phi f= \int_0^{+\infty} \widetilde{P}_\tau f g_S(\tau;s)\, d\tau
    \end{equation*}
    and denote by $\left(-\phi(-(G-\lambda I)), {\sf Dom}(\phi(-(G-\lambda I)))\right)$ its generator. By Phillips formula (see \cite[Theorem \textcolor{blue}{12}.6]{schilling2009bernstein}) we know that ${\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I) \subset {\sf Dom}(\phi(-(G-\lambda I)))$ and on $f \in {\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)$ it holds
    \begin{equation*}
        -\phi(-(G-\lambda I))f=-\int_0^\infty \left(e^{-\lambda_\star s}P_sf-f\right) \nu_\phi(ds).
    \end{equation*}
    Since $\widetilde{q} \in {\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)$, we get by (\ref{eq:equality2})
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:equality3}
        -\phi(-(G-\lambda I))\widetilde{q}=0.
    \end{equation}
    Finally, notice that for all $f \in {\sf Dom}(G-\lambda I)$ it holds
    \begin{equation*}
    \left\Vert \widetilde{P}_s^\phi f \right\Vert_{C(E)}\le  \int_0^{+\infty} \left\Vert \widetilde{P}_\tau f \right\Vert_{C(E)} g_S(\tau;s)\, d\tau \le e^{-s\phi(\lambda)}\left\Vert f \right\Vert_{C(E)},
    \end{equation*}
    where $\phi(\lambda)>0$. Hence, by the Hille-Yosida Theorem (see \cite[Theorems 3.3.2 and 3.3.4]{arendt2011vector}) we know that $0$ belongs to the resolvent set of $-\phi(\lambda I-G)$ and thus the operator is invertible. As a consequence, (\ref{eq:equality3}) implies
    \begin{equation*}
        \widetilde{q}(\lambda,\cdot) \equiv 0.
    \end{equation*}
    By injectivity of the Laplace transform (see \cite[Theorem 1.7.3]{arendt2011vector}), this ends the proof.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- rmk-3: body_md -->
Notice that the function $q$ in Proposition \ref{prop:unique2} is not a solution of (\ref{eq:q}) in the sense of Definition \ref{def:sol}. Indeed, on the one hand we are not requiring that $q$ is a continuous function of $(t,x)$, on the other hand $q$ solves (\ref{eq:q}) not for all $(t,x) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E$ but only on a subset $\widetilde{E} \subset \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \times E$ such that for all $x \in E$ the set $\widetilde{I}_x=\{t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+: \ (t,x) \not \in \widetilde{E}\}$ satisfies $|\widetilde{I}_x|=0$. With an abuse of notation, we could refer to such a function $q$ as an \textit{almost-everywhere (a.e.) solution} of (\ref{eq:q}). From this point of view, Proposition \ref{prop:unique2} tells us that the solution provided in {Theorem} \ref{thm:main1} is also the unique a.e. solution satisfying some additional conditions on the Laplace transform. Finally, notice that we are not requiring that $\lim_{x \to \infty}q(t,x)=0$ locally uniformly with respect to $t \ge 0$.
<!-- end rmk-3 -->
<!-- thm-5: body_md -->
For $t \ge 0$ set $\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_t=\operatorname{\mathcal{G}}_{L(t)-}$. Then the process $(X_{\rm e},\gamma)$ is a time-homogeneous Markov process with respect to the filtration $\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}:=\{\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_t\}_{t \ge 0}$.
<!-- end thm-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
The proof is exactly the same of the ones of \cite[Theorems 3.2 and 4.1]{meerschaert2014semi}, with the only exception that since $(B^\phi,{S})$ is not a canonical Feller process in our setting, one has to modify the translation operator accordingly. Precisely, in our case we just need to set $\theta_t \omega(s)=(\omega_1(s),\omega_2(s+t))$ for all $t,s \ge 0$. Indeed, for $s,t \ge 0$ and $\omega \in \Omega$
    \begin{multline*}
        (B^\phi(\theta_t\omega,s),S(\theta_t\omega,s))=((B(\theta_t\omega),S(\theta_t\omega,s)),S(\theta_t\omega,s))\\
        =
        (\omega_1(\omega_2(t+s)),\omega_2(t+s))=(B^\phi(\omega,t+s),S(\omega,t+s)).
    \end{multline*}
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lem-2: body_md -->
\label{lemmamart}
For any {$m \in \mathbb{R}$} and $x,v \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$, the process
\begin{equation*}
    Z^m(t):=e^{mX_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{m^2 H(t)}{2}}
\end{equation*}
is a $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}$-martingale under $\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}$.
<!-- end lem-2 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
$Z^m$ is $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}$-adapted by definition. To show that  $Z^m(t) \in L^1(\Omega;\mathds{P}^{(x,v)})$ for all $t \ge 0$, just notice that
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[Z^m(t)]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)(H(t)) \mid H(t)\right]\right],
\end{equation*}
where
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)(\tau)=e^{mB(\tau)-\frac{m^2\tau}{2}}
\end{equation*}
is the Doleans-Dade exponential of $mB$. Since $B$ and $H$ are independent, we have
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)(H(t)) \mid H(t)\right]=F_1(H(t)),
\end{equation*}
where
\begin{equation*}
F_1(\tau):=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)(\tau)\right]= {\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,0)}\left[\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)(\tau)\right] = e^x}.
\end{equation*}
Hence
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[Z^m(t)] = {e^x \int_{0}^{t-v} \, \overline{\nu}_\phi(t-v-s)u^\phi(s) \, ds = e^x<\infty}.
\end{equation*}
To show the martingale property, fix $0 \le s \le t$ and consider two Borel sets $A_1,A_2 \subset D_-[0,s]$. Consider the event
\begin{equation*}
    A_\star:=\{{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  } \in A_1\} \cap \{{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  } \in A_2\}. 
\end{equation*}
Since $B$ and $H$ are independent and $\gamma(t)=t-v-H(t)$ for all $t \ge 0$, it holds
\begin{equation}\label{eq:DDexpo1}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[Z^m(t)\mathds{1}_{A_\star}]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{A_2}(\gamma)F_2({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,t]}                        
  })],
\end{equation}
where, for all $g \in \Lambda_-[0,t]$, we set
\begin{align*}
F_2(g)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  B(g(\cdot))               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)(g(t))\right] \ \mbox{ and } \ ,
\end{align*}
and we notice that
\begin{equation*}
F_2({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,t]}                        
  })=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[Z^m(t)\mathds{1}_{A_1}(X_{\rm e}) \mid H(\tau), \ \tau \ge 0].
\end{equation*}
Notice in particular that $\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)$ is the Doleans-Dade exponential of $mB$. Since $A_1 \subset D_-[0,s]$, the event $\{{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  B(g(\cdot))               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  } \in A_1\}$ belongs to $\sigma(B(w), \ w \le g(s))$. Furthermore, once we recall that $\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)$ is a martingale and $g(s) \le g(t)$, we have
\begin{equation*}
F_2(g)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}(B(g(\cdot)))\operatorname{\mathcal{E}}(mB)(g(s))\right]=:\widetilde{F}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  g               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  }).
\end{equation*}
However, we have that, still by independence between $B$ and $H$
\begin{equation*}
\widetilde{F}_2({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[Z^m(s)\mathds{1}_{A_1}(X_{\rm e}) \mid H(\tau), \ \tau \ge 0].
\end{equation*}
Substituting it back into (\ref{eq:DDexpo1}), we get
\begin{equation}\label{eq:DDexpo2}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[Z^m(t)\mathds{1}_{A_\star}]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[\mathds{1}_{A_2}(\gamma)\widetilde{F}_2({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[Z^m(s)\mathds{1}_{A_\star}].
\end{equation}
Since the events of the form $A_\star$ constitute a $\pi$-system generating $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}_s$, this ends the proof.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
For any $(x,v) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^2$, the process $X$ is a{n} $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}$-martingale under $\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}$.
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
By definition $X$ is $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}$-adapted. Now we show that for all $t \in [0,T]$ it is true that $X(t) \in L^1(\Omega;\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)})$. To do this, first notice that
\begin{align*}
		\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}[X(t)]&=e^{\frac{x}{2}}\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]\\
		&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid H(t),H(T)\right]\right].
\end{align*}
Since $H(t) \le H(T)$ are independent of the Brownian motion $B$, if we set, for $s_1 \le s_2$,
\begin{equation*}
F_1(s_1,s_2)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{B(s_1)-\frac{B(s_2)}{2}+\frac{x}{2}-\frac{s_2}{8}}\right]
\end{equation*}
we get
\begin{align*}
	\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}[X(t)]&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[F_1(H(t),H(T))\right].
\end{align*}
However, by Girsanov's theorem (see \cite[Theorem 5.1]{karatzas2014brownian}), we know that $\{B(s)+\frac{s}{2}, \ s \in [0,s_2]\}$ is a Brownian motion under the measure 
\begin{equation*}
A \in \operatorname{\mathcal{F}}_{s_2} \mapsto \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_Ae^{-\frac{B(s_2)}{2}+\frac{x}{2}-\frac{s_2}{8}}\right],
\end{equation*}
hence in particular $e^{B(s)}=e^{B(s)+\frac{s}{2}-\frac{s}{2}}$ is the Doleans-Dade exponential of $B(s)+\frac{s}{2}$ and thus a martingale under the same measure. As a consequence, we have that
\begin{equation*}
F_1(s_1,s_2)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{B(s_1)-\frac{B(s_2)}{2}+\frac{x}{2}-\frac{s_2}{8}}\right]=e^{x}
\end{equation*}
and then
\begin{align*}
	\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}[X(t)]&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[F_1(H(t),H(T))\right]=e^x<\infty.
\end{align*}


                                                                            
                                                                            
                
                                                                                                                
                                                                                                                        
              
                                                                    
                   
                                                                                                
                 
                                                
	Now we need to prove the martingale property. To do this, fix {$s$ and $t$ such that} $0 \le s \le t$ and let $A_1,A_2 \in D_-[0,s]$ be Borel sets. Define the event
 \begin{equation*}
     A_{\star}:=\{{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  } \in A_1\} \cap \{{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  } \in A_2\}.
 \end{equation*}
Then we have, for any $T>t$,
\begin{align}
&\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}\left[X(t)\mathds{1}_{A_\star}\right]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })\mathds{1}_{A_2}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]\nonumber\\
&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_2}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })F_2({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  } {)}\right]\label{eq:premartingale},
	\end{align}
where for any $g \in \Lambda_-[0,T]$ we set
\begin{equation*}
    F_2(g):=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  B(g(\cdot))               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })e^{\frac{x}{2}+B(g(t))-\frac{B(g(T))}{2}-\frac{g(T)}{8}}\right]
\end{equation*}
and we notice that
\begin{equation*}
    F_2({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  })=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid \ {                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right].
\end{equation*}
Since $A_1 \subset D_-[0,s]$, the event $\{{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  B(g(\cdot))               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  } \in A_1\}$ belongs to $\sigma(B(w), \ w \le g(s))$. Therefore we use again Girsanov's theorem and use the martingale property of Brownian (conditionally on the independent $H|_{[0,T]}$) to get now
\begin{equation*}
    F_2(g)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  B(g(\cdot))               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })e^{\frac{x}{2}+B(g(s))-\frac{B(g(T))}{2}-\frac{g(T)}{8}}\right].
\end{equation*}
In particular, we have that
\begin{equation*}
    F_2({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  })=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(s)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid \ {                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right].
\end{equation*}
When we replace the latter equality back into (\ref{eq:premartingale}), we obtain
\begin{align}
\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}&\left[X(t)\mathds{1}_{A_\star}\right]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })\mathds{1}_{A_2}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(t)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]\nonumber\\
&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A_1}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })\mathds{1}_{A_2}({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,s]}                        
  })e^{\frac{x}{2}+X_{\rm e}(s)-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]=\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}\left[X(s)\mathds{1}_{A_\star}\right].
\end{align}
Given that events of the form of $A_\star$ are a $\pi$-system generating $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}_s$, this completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:Markov}
	The process $(X,\gamma)$ is $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}$-Markov under $\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}$.
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Consider any function $f \in L^\infty(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^2)$ and let $0 \le s \le t$. We have, for any $T>t$,
	                                                            
	\begin{align*}
		\widetilde{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid \operatorname{\mathcal{N}}_s\right]&= { \frac{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{X_e (T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid \operatorname{\mathcal{N}}_s\right]}{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{X_e(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\mid \operatorname{\mathcal{N}}_s\right]}}\\
        &=\frac{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid \operatorname{\mathcal{N}}_s\right]}{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid \operatorname{\mathcal{N}}_s\right]}\\
		&=\frac{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]}{\mathds{E}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}}\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right]}\\
		&=\widetilde{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s),\gamma(s)\right],
	\end{align*}
	where we used the fact that $H(T)=T-v-\gamma(T)$ and $X_{\rm e}(T)=\log(X(T))$ under $\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}$ and the $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}$-Markov property of $(X,\gamma)$ with respect to $\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:timehomo1}
Let $0 \le s \le t$. Then, for all $x,x^\prime \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ and $v,v^\prime \le s$ it holds
\begin{equation}\label{eq:equalityexp}
	\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s), \ \gamma(s)\right]=\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x^\prime,v^\prime)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))\mid X(s), \ \gamma(s)\right].
\end{equation}
In particular, one can define, for $0 \le s \le t$, the transition semigroup $\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}$ of $(X,\gamma)$ under $\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}$ acting on bounded measurable functions $f:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ in such a way that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transsemi1}
\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}f(X(s),\gamma(s))=\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t)) \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right],
\end{equation}
for any $x \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ and $v \le s$. Furthermore, $\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}$ only depends on $t-s$ and then $(X,\gamma)$ is time-homogeneous.
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Let $(Q_{t})_{t \ge 0}$ be the transition semigroup under $\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}$ of the time-homogeneous process $(X,\gamma)$, i.e. for all bounded measurable functions $f:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ and for all $t \ge 0$, $Q_tf:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ is the bounded measurable function such that for any $s \ge 0$, $x \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+$ and $v \in [0,s]$ 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:semigroupQ}
    Q_tf(X(s),\gamma(s))=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[f(X(t+s),\gamma(t+s)) \mid X(s), \gamma(s)].
\end{equation}
The independence on $(x,v)$ is justified by observing that the transition semigroup of $(X_{\rm e},\gamma)$ is already independent of $(x,v)$ by \cite[Theorems 3.2 and 4.1]{meerschaert2014semi}. 

Notice that (\ref{eq:semigroupQ}) holds whenever $f:\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+ \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ is a measurable function such that
\begin{equation*}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[|f(X(t),\gamma(t))|\right]<\infty
\end{equation*}
for all $t \ge 0$ and $x,v \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$. Now fix $0 \le s \le t$, $x,x^\prime \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+$ and $v,v^\prime \le s$. Then, {as a consequence of Proposition \ref{prop:Markov}, for any $T>t$,} we have
\begin{align}\label{eq:prehomo0}
    \widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t)) \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right]=\frac{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-\textcolor{blue}{v -}\gamma(T)}{8}} \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right]}{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-\textcolor{blue}{v -}\gamma(T)}{8}} \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right]}.
\end{align}
To deal with the numerator note that, by Lemma \ref{lemmamart}, we can use the martingale property to get, for any $y$, $w$ and $T>t$, that for the Markov semigroup $Q_t$, $t \geq 0$, it is true that
\begin{align}
    Q_{T-t}f(y,w) = f(y,w).
\end{align}
Hence, for the numerator in (\ref{eq:prehomo0}), we have
\begin{align}
\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}} \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))Q_{T-t}g(X(t),\gamma(t)) \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right], \label{eq:pretimehomo1}
\end{align}
where 
\begin{equation*}
    g(y,w)=e^{-\frac{\log(y)}{2}-\frac{T{-S(0)}-w}{8}}.
\end{equation*}
Next, let
\begin{equation*}
    h(y,w)=f(y,w)Q_{T-t}g(y,w).
\end{equation*}
Given that $f$ is bounded, we can write 
\begin{align*}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[|h(X(t),\gamma(t))|\right] &\le \left(\sup_{(y,w) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+\times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+}|f(y,w)|\right)\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[g(X(T),\gamma(T))\right]\\
    &=e^x\left(\sup_{(y,w) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+\times \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+}|f(y,w)|\right)<\infty.
\end{align*}
Hence (\ref{eq:pretimehomo1}) can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pretimehomo2}
    \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t))e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}} \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right]=Q_{t-s}h(X(s),\gamma(s)).
\end{equation}
For the denominator of (\ref{eq:prehomo0}), we have
\begin{align*}
&\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[e^{-\frac{\log(X(T))}{2}-\frac{T-v-\gamma(T)}{8}} \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right]=Q_{T-s}g(X(s),\gamma(s)).
\end{align*}
Hence, (\ref{eq:prehomo0}) can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transsemi1.5}
\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}\left[f(X(t),\gamma(t)) \mid X(s), \gamma(s)\right]=\frac{Q_{t-s}h(X(s),\gamma(s))}{Q_{T-s}g(X(s),\gamma(s))}.
\end{equation}
The right-hand side is independent of the choice of $(x,v)$, therefore we get (\ref{eq:equalityexp}). Now, let us select $T=t+1$, so that $h(y,w)=f(y,w)Q_{1}g(t,w)$, and let us set
\begin{equation*}
\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}f(y,w)=\frac{Q_{t-s}\left(f(\cdot,\cdot)Q_{1}g(\cdot,\cdot)\right)(y,w)}{Q_{t-s+1}g(y,w)}.
\end{equation*}
Then we see that $\widetilde{Q}_{s,t}$ only depends on $t-s$ and (\ref{eq:transsemi1}) holds by virtue of (\ref{eq:transsemi1.5}).
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:CameronMartin}
For any $(x,v) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^2$ and any $T>0$, the distribution of ${                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2},\gamma\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }$ under $\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}$ coincides with the one of ${                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(X_{\rm e},\gamma\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }$ under $\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}$. As a consequence, the distribution of $(X,\gamma)$ under $\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}$ coincides with the one of $\left(Xe^{-\frac{H}{2}},\gamma\right)$ under $\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}$.
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Fix $T>0$ and consider a bounded and measurable function $f:D_-[0,T] \times \Lambda_-[0,T] \to \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$. Then we have
\begin{align}
	\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}&\left[f\left({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2}\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  },{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right)\right]\\
 &=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f\left({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2}\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  },{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right)e^{-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}}\right]\nonumber \\
	&=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[F({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  })\right]\label{eq:predist},
\end{align} 
 where we set, for any $g \in \Lambda_-[0,T]$,
 \begin{equation*}
     \widetilde{\gamma}(g)(t):=t-v-g(t) \ \mbox{ and } \  F(g)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f\left({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(B(g(\cdot))+\frac{g(\cdot)}{2}\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  },{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \widetilde{\gamma}(g)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right)e^{-\frac{B(g(T))}{2}-\frac{g(T)}{8}}\right]
 \end{equation*}
and we notice that
\begin{equation*}
    F({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  })=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f\left({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2}\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  },{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right)e^{-\frac{X_{\rm e}(T)}{2}-\frac{H(T)}{8}} \mid {                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right].
\end{equation*}
By Girsanov theorem, however, we know that under the measure
\begin{equation*}
A \in \operatorname{\mathcal{F}}_{g(T)} \mapsto \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}e^{-\frac{B(g(T))}{2}-\frac{g(T)}{8}}\right]
\end{equation*}
the process $t \in [0,g(T)] \mapsto B(t)+\frac{t}{2}$ is a Brownian motion. Hence, the distribution of ${                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(B(g(\cdot))+\frac{g(\cdot)}{2}\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }$ under the aforementioned measure coincides with the distribution of ${                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  B(g(\cdot))               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }$ under $\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}$ and then
\begin{equation*}
    F(g)=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  B(g(\cdot))               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  },{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \widetilde{\gamma}(g)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  })\right].
\end{equation*}
In particular, we notice that, by independence of $B$ and $H$,
\begin{equation*}
    F({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  })=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  },{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }) \mid {                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  H               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right].
\end{equation*}
Substituting this into (\ref{eq:predist}) we have
\begin{align}
	\widetilde{\operatorname{\mathds{E}}}^{(x,v)}\left[f\left({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2}\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  },{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }\right)\right]=\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}\left[f({                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  X_{\rm e}               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  },{                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \gamma               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  })\right].
\end{align} 
Since $f$ is arbitrary, we have the equality in distribution.

Next, notice that with the same argument as in Proposition \ref{prop:Markov}, one can prove that $\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2},\gamma\right)$ is a $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}$-Markov process under $\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}$. Recalling also that $(X_{\rm e},\gamma)$ is $\operatorname{\mathcal{N}}$-Markov with respect to the measure $\operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}$, we have that since the distribution of ${                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  \left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2},\gamma\right)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }$ under $\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}$ and ${                                            
  \left.\kern-\nulldelimiterspace                                           
  (X_{\rm e},\gamma)               
  \vphantom{\big|}                                              
  \right|_{[0,T]}                        
  }$ under $\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}$ coincide for all $T>0$, then the distribution of the whole trajectories must coincide. Finally, the distribution of $(X,\gamma)$ under $\widetilde{\mathds{P}}^{(x,v)}$ coincides with the one of $\left(Xe^{-\frac{H}{2}},\gamma\right)$ under $\mathds{P}^{(x,v)}$ since both of them are expressed as deterministic functions of $\left(X_{\rm e}+\frac{H}{2},\gamma\right)$ and $\left(X_{\rm e},\gamma\right)$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- rmk-4: body_md -->
\label{rem:alphadist}
 The undershoot of an inverse stable subordinator is particularly tractable, in that its marginal distributions are known. Indeed, the law of $H(t)$ coincides with that of $t B$ where $B$ is a Beta$(\beta,1-\ \beta)$ random variable (\cite{meercoupled}, Example 5.5). Based on this, after the usual conditioning argument it can be deduced that the characteristic function $\varphi_{X(t)}(z)$ of $X(t)$ is given by 
\begin{equation*}
    \varphi_{X(t)}(z)=_1\hspace{-.1cm}F_1(\alpha, t/2) , \qquad z \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}},
\end{equation*}
where $_1F_1$ is the confluent hypergeometric function. The above follows as a particular case of \cite{torricelli}, Theorem 6.2, (6.4), second line.
<!-- end rmk-4 -->
<!-- lem-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:momgen}
    It holds
    \begin{align*}
        \operatorname{\mathds{E}}^{(x,v)}[e^{\lambda_1 |B(t)|+\lambda_2B(t)}]&=e^{(\lambda_1+\lambda_2) x+\frac{(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)^2}{2}t}\Phi\left(-\frac{(\lambda_1+\lambda_2) t+x}{\sqrt{t}}\right)\\
        &\qquad +e^{-(\lambda_1-\lambda_2) x+\frac{(\lambda_1-\lambda_2)^2}{2}t}\Phi\left(-\frac{(\lambda_1-\lambda_2) t-x}{\sqrt{t}}\right)
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lem-3 -->
<!-- lem-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:BSuniqueness}
	For all $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ there exists a unique classical solution $u_f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol}$ of 
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:BSclass1}
		\begin{cases}
			\partial_t u(t,x)=Gu(t,x), & t>0, \ x \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \\
			u(0,x)=f(x), & x>0,
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation}
	i.e., $(\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0,\operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm sol})$ is a uniqueness class for $G$. If we denote by $(P_t)_{t \geq 0}$ the associated semigroup action, then the following properties hold true.
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item For all $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ we have that 
		\begin{align}
			P_t f (x) \, = \,  \int_0^{+\infty} f(y) p_{\rm GBM}(t,y;x)dy
		\end{align}
	where {GBM stands for geometric Brownian motion and}
	\begin{equation}\label{heatker}
		p_{\rm GBM}(t,y;x):=x^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{t}{8}}\frac{p(t,\log(y)-\log(x))}{y^{\frac{3}{2}}} \ \mbox{ and } \ p(t,w)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}e^{-\frac{w^2}{2t}}.
	\end{equation}
        As a consequence, the semigroup $(P_t)_{t \ge 0}$ is Markovian and positivity preserving.
	\item Let $\beta \ge 0$ and assume $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(\beta)$. Then $\partial_x P_tf \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(\beta+1)$ and 
	\begin{equation*}
		[\partial_x P_tf(x)]_{\beta+1} \le 4[f]_\beta\exp\left(\beta\frac{t}{2}+\frac{\beta^2 t^2}{2}\right).
	\end{equation*}
	\item For all $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0 \cap \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}$ such that $\partial_x f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ and $Gf \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ it holds
	\begin{equation*}
		P_tGf=GP_tf.
	\end{equation*}
        \item Fix $\beta_1,\beta_2 \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$ and let $f_{\beta_1,\beta_2}(x)=e^{\beta_1|\log(x)|+\beta_2\log(x)}$. Then
            \begin{align*}
                P_tf_{\beta_1,\beta_2}(x)&=e^{(\beta_1+\beta_2) \log(x)+\frac{(\beta_1+\beta_2)(\beta_1+\beta_2-1)}{2}t}\Phi\left(-\left(\beta_1+\beta_2-\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{t}-\frac{\log(x)}{\sqrt{t}}\right)\\
                &\qquad + e^{-(\beta_1-\beta_2)\log(x)+\frac{(\beta_1-\beta_2)(\beta_1-\beta_2+1)}{2}t}\Phi\left(-\left(\beta_1-\beta_2+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{t}+\frac{\log(x)}{\sqrt{t}}\right).
            \end{align*}
        \item If $f \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(\beta)$, then $P_tf \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(\beta)$ with
        \begin{equation*}
            [P_tf]_{\beta} \le 2[f]_{\beta}e^{\frac{\beta(\beta+1)}{2}t}.
        \end{equation*}
                                                                                                                            
                   
                                                                                                                                                      
                 
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-4 -->
<!-- lem-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:qBS}
	For all $t,x>0$ it holds
	\begin{align}
		\partial_x q_{\rm BS}(t,x)&=\Phi\left(\frac{2\log(x/K)+t}{2\sqrt{t}}\right), \label{eq:der1}\\
		\partial^2_x q_{\rm BS}(t,x)&=\sqrt{\frac{K}{2\pi tx^3}}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^2\left(x/K\right)+t^2}{8t}\right), \label{eq:der2}\\
		\partial_t q_{\rm BS}(t,x)&=G{q}_{\rm BS}(t,x)=\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi t}}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^2\left(x/K\right)+t^2}{8t}\right). \label{eq:der3}
	\end{align}
	In particular,
	\begin{align}
            \left|q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right| &\le e^{|\log(x)|} \label{eq:der3.5}\\
		\left|\partial_x q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right|&\le 1, \label{eq:der4}\\
		\left|\partial_t q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right|&=\left|G q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right| \le \sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi t}}.\label{eq:der5}
	\end{align}
	Furthermore, for all $a>0$ and $x \ge a$ we have
	\begin{equation}
		\left|\partial_x^2 q_{\rm BS}(t,x)\right| \le \sqrt{\frac{K}{8\pi a^3 t}}. \label{eq:der6}
	\end{equation}
        In particular $q_{BS}$ is the unique solution of
        	\begin{equation}\label{eq:BSclass1}
		\begin{cases}
			\partial_t q_{\rm BS}(t,x)=Gq_{\rm BS}(t,x), & t>0, \ x \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \\
			q_{\rm BS}(0,x)=(x-K)_+, & x>0,\\
                q_{\rm BS} \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{0,  {\rm loc}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+).
		\end{cases}
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-5 -->
<!-- thm-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:main2}
Let $\phi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}$ for $\alpha \in \left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)$. Let also, for any $h \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$, $A \in \operatorname{\mathcal{B}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}})$ and $w \ge 0$
\begin{align}\label{eq:K}
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(A,h)=\int_0^{+\infty}\mathds{1}_A(s)p(s,h)\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds \qquad \mathcal{K}(d\tau,dh)=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(d\tau,h)dh
\end{align}
and
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{K}_w(A)=\begin{cases}
    \displaystyle \frac{\mathcal{K}(A \cap (\operatorname{\mathbb{R}} \times [w,+\infty)))}{\mathcal{K}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}} \times [w,+\infty))} & w>0\\[7pt]
    \delta_{(0,0)}(A) & w=0.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Then
\begin{equation*}
q_\star(t,x,0)=q(T-t,x)
\end{equation*}
and, for $w>0$ and any $v<t-w$,
\begin{multline}\label{eq:possoj}
q_\star(t,x,w)=\left(xe^{-\frac{t-v-w}{2}}-K\right)_+\mathcal{K}_w(x;\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^d \times [w+T-t,\infty))\\
    +\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}} \times [w,w+T-t)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\! q(T-t+w-\tau,\log(x)+y+t-w+\tau-v)\mathcal{K}_w(d\tau,dy),
\end{multline}
where $q$ is the unique solution of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:BSUnder}
    \begin{cases}
        (\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(t,x)=\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}q(0,x) \\
        q(0,x)=(x-K)_+\\
        q \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{0, {\rm loc}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+).
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
<!-- end thm-6 -->
<!-- rmk-5: body_md -->
Notice that (\ref{eq:possoj}) is a formula that determines the value of $q_\star$ uniquely for positive sojourn time. Indeed, the only quantity that is involved is the function $q$ which is related to the value of $q_\star$ on renewal states, i.e. for zero sojourn time, which is the unique solution with at most power growth at $0$ and $\infty$ of the fully nonlocal Black-Scholes equation (\ref{eq:BSUnder}). This is also underlined by the fact that (\ref{eq:possoj}) can be rewritten as
\begin{multline}\label{eq:possoj2}
q_\star(t,x,w)=\left(xe^{-\frac{t-v-w}{2}}-K\right)_+\mathcal{K}_w(x;\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^d \times [w+T-t,\infty))\\
    +\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{R}} \times [w,w+T-t)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\! q_\star(t-w+\tau,\log(x)+y+t-w+\tau-v,0)\mathcal{K}_w(d\tau,dy),
\end{multline}
that only involves the function $q_\star(t,x,0)$, which is given by the solution of (\ref{eq:BSUnder}).{We remark that $(x-K)_+$ appears as an initial condition in (\ref{eq:BSUnder}) but, given that the fair price of our call option at time $t>0$ is $q_\star(t,x,w)$, then 
    \begin{align}
        (x-K)_+=q(0,x)  \, = \, q_\star (T,x,0)
    \end{align}
and thus $(x-K)_+$ is the usual final condition for
$q_\star(\cdot,\cdot,0)$. It follows that, according to our model, the fair price at time $t>0$ of a(n) (intraday) plain vanilla call option (with zero interest rate, strike price $K$ and maturity $T>t$) can be determined by solving the initial value problem (\ref{eq:BSUnder}) then changing the variable $t \rightsquigarrow  T-t$ and finally using (\ref{eq:possoj2}).
}
<!-- end rmk-5 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:ACqstar}
	Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:main2}, for all $x>0$ the function $q(\cdot,x) \in {\rm AC}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+)$ and its a.e. derivative $\partial_t q(\cdot,x)$ satisfies, for a.a. $t \ge 0$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:ACqstar}
		|\partial_t q(t,x)| \le \frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{t}}.
	\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
For any $t > 0$, let $h \in \left(-\frac{t}{2},1\right)$ and consider $D^hq(t,x)=\frac{q(t+h,x)-q(t,x)}{h}$. Then we have, by (\ref{eq:selfsim}),
\begin{equation*}
	|D^h q(t,x)| \le \int_0^1 \left|\frac{q_{\rm BS}(ts+hs,x)-q_{\rm BS}(ts,x)}{hs}\right|\frac{s^{\alpha}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds.
\end{equation*}
By Lagrange theorem, we know that there exists $\xi \in [\min\{ts,ts+hs\},\max\{ts,ts+hs\}]$ such that
\begin{equation*}
	\left|\frac{q_{\rm BS}(ts+hs,x)-q_{\rm BS}(ts,x)}{hs}\right|=\left|\partial_t q_{\rm BS}(x,\xi)\right|\le  \sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi \xi}} \le \sqrt{\frac{Kx}{4\pi ts}},
\end{equation*}
where we also used (\ref{eq:der5}). Hence
\begin{equation*}
	|D^hq(t,x)| \le \sqrt{\frac{Kx}{4\pi t}}\int_0^1 \frac{s^{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds=\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{t}}.
                                                                    
\end{equation*}
The right-hand side is independent of $h$ and belongs to $L^1(0,T)$ for all $T>0$, thus we can argue as in Proposition~\ref{prop:AC} and get the result.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- coro-3: body_md -->
\label{coro:diffest}
    Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main2} we have that for all $t,x>0$
    \begin{equation*}
    \int_0^t |P_s q(t-s,x)-P_sq(t,x)|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds<\infty
    \end{equation*}
    and, for all $\lambda>\frac{3}{8}$ and $x>0$,
    \begin{equation*}
    \int_0^\infty \int_0^t e^{-\lambda t}  |P_s q(t-s,x)-P_sq(t,x)|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds\, dt<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end coro-3 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Fix $x>0$, and note that for $0 <s<t$,
    \begin{multline}
     \left|   q (t-s,x) - q (t,x) \right| \leq \int_0^s \left| \partial_t q(t-\tau,x) \right| d\tau \\
     \leq  \frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{Kx}\int_0^s(t-\tau)^{-1/2}d\tau
     =\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right) s}{\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{t-s}}.\label{623}
    \end{multline}
    This proves that $\left|   q (t-s,\cdot) - q (t,\cdot) \right| \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(1/2)$. We get, by also employing Items $(1)$ and $(5)$ of Lemma \ref{lem:BSuniqueness},
    \begin{align*}
        \int_0^t &|P_s q(t-s,x)-P_sq(t,x)|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds\\
        & \le \int_0^t P_s |q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)|(x)\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds \\
        &\le \frac{2 \alpha \Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right) \sqrt{K}}{\pi \Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|+\frac{3}{8}t}\int_0^t s^{-\alpha}(t-s)^{-\frac{1}{2}}ds\\
        &=\frac{2 \alpha \Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right) \sqrt{K}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\alpha\right)\sqrt{\pi} }t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|+\frac{3}{8}t},
    \end{align*}
    where we also used \cite[Lemma 1.11]{ascione2023fractional}. Furthermore, for any $\lambda>\frac{3}{8}$ it holds
    \begin{align*}
        \int_0^\infty &\int_0^t e^{-\lambda t}  |P_s q(t-s,x)-P_sq(t,x)|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds\, dt \\
        &\le \frac{2 \alpha \Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right) \sqrt{K}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\alpha\right)\sqrt{\pi} }t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\int_0^\infty t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}e^{-\left(\lambda-\frac{3}{8}\right)t}\, dt\\
        &=\frac{2 \alpha \Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right) \sqrt{K}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\sqrt{\pi} }t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\left(\lambda-\frac{3}{8}\right)^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- prop-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:regq}
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main2}, the following equalities hold for all $t,x>0$:
\begin{align}
    &\partial_x q (t,x) \, = \, \frac{1}{\Gamma (\alpha)\Gamma (1-\alpha)}\int_0^1 \partial_x q_{\rm BS} (\tau t, x) \, \tau^{\alpha-1} (1-\tau)^{-\alpha} d\tau , \label{derstar1}\\
    & \partial_x^2 q(t,x) \, = \, \frac{1}{\Gamma (\alpha)\Gamma (1-\alpha)}\int_0^1 \partial_x^2 q_{\rm BS} (\tau t, x) \, \tau^{\alpha-1} (1-\tau)^{-\alpha} d\tau , \label{derstar2}\\
    & G q (t,x) \, = \, \frac{1}{\Gamma (\alpha)\Gamma (1-\alpha)}\int_0^1 G q_{\rm BS} (\tau t, x) \, \tau^{\alpha-1} (1-\tau)^{-\alpha} d\tau . \label{derstar3}
\end{align}
Furthermore $q(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(1)$, $\partial_x q(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(0)$ and $Gq(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(1/2)$ for all $t>0$, where
\begin{align}
|q(t,x)| &\le e^{|\log(x)|} \label{eq:est1q}\\
|\partial_x q(t,x)| &\le 1 \label{eq:est2q}\\
|G q(t,x)| & \le  \sqrt{\frac{K x}{2 t}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)} \label{eq:est3q}.
\end{align}
In particular, $q \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_{0, {\rm loc}}(\operatorname{\mathbb{R}}_0^+)$.
<!-- end prop-11 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
By (\ref{eq:qspecial}) and (\ref{eq:der3.5}) we already know that $q \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(1)$ with $[q]_1 \le 1$. Moreover, by (\ref{eq:der4}), we know that we can apply the dominated convergence theorem to take the derivative inside the integral sign in (\ref{eq:qspecial}) and then using (\ref{eq:selfsim}), getting (\ref{derstar1}). Furthermore, (\ref{eq:der4}) also implies that $\partial_x q(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(0)$ with $[\partial_x q(t,\cdot)]_0 \le 1$. 

To argue with the second derivative, fix $a > 0$ and notice that for all $x \ge a$ it holds, by (\ref{eq:der6}),
\begin{align*}
    \int_0^1 \left|\partial_x^2 q_{\rm BS}(ts,x)\right|\frac{s^{\alpha-1}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds &\le \sqrt{\frac{K}{8\pi a^3 t}}\int_0^1 \frac{s^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds\\
    &=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{K}{2a^3 t}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}<\infty,
\end{align*}
since $\alpha>\frac{1}{2}$. Hence, by dominated convergence, we get (\ref{derstar2}) and finally, multiplying both sides of (\ref{derstar2}) by $\frac{x^2}{2}$, we also get (\ref{derstar3}). Finally, notice that (\ref{derstar3}) and (\ref{eq:der5}) imply that $Gq(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(1/2)$ for all $t>0$ and in particular
\begin{equation*}
\left|Gq(t,x)\right| \le \sqrt{\frac{K x}{8\pi t}}\int_0^1 \frac{s^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds=\sqrt{\frac{K x}{2 t}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- rmk-6: body_md -->
Notice that Proposition \ref{prop:ACqstar}, Corollary \ref{coro:diffest}, (\ref{derstar1}), (\ref{eq:est1q}) and (\ref{eq:est2q}) hold for any $\alpha \in (0,1)$. The condition $\alpha>1/2$ comes into play only for (\ref{derstar2}), (\ref{derstar3}) and (\ref{eq:est3q}). However, it is worth highlighting that, using a finer estimate in place of (\ref{eq:der6}), it is possible to prove that (\ref{derstar2}) and (\ref{derstar3}) still hold for all $t, x>0$ with $x \not = K$ if $\alpha \le 1/2$. However, notice that, for $h \in [0,1]$, by Lagrange's theorem,
\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial_x q_{\rm BS}(ts,K+h)-\partial_xq_{\rm BS}(ts,K)}{h}&=\partial_x^{2}q_{\rm BS}(ts,\xi)\\
&=\sqrt{\frac{K}{2\pi ts \xi^3}}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^2(\xi/K)+t^2s^2}{8ts}\right)\\
&\ge \sqrt{\frac{K}{2\pi ts (K+1)^3}}\exp\left(-\frac{4\log^2\left(1+\frac{h}{K}\right)+t^2s^2}{8ts}\right)
\end{align*}
where $\xi \in [K,K+h]$. Hence, by (\ref{derstar1}), we have
\begin{align*}
&\frac{\partial_x q(t,K+h)-\partial_xq(t,K)}{h}\\
&\qquad =\int_0^1 \frac{\partial_x q_{\rm BS}(ts,K+h)-\partial_xq_{\rm BS}(ts,K)}{h}\frac{s^{\alpha-1}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha) \Gamma(1-\alpha)}\, ds\\
&\qquad \ge \frac{\sqrt{K}}{\sqrt{2\pi t(K+1)^3}}\int_0^1 \exp\left(-\frac{4\log^2\left(1+\frac{h}{K}\right)+t^2s^2}{8ts}\right) \frac{s^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha) \Gamma(1-\alpha)}\, ds.
\end{align*}
Taking the limit as $h \to 0^+$ we have, as a consequence of the monotone convergence theorem,
\begin{multline*}
\lim_{h \to 0^+}\frac{\partial_x q(t,K+h)-\partial_xq(t,K)}{h}\\
\ge \frac{\sqrt{K}}{\Gamma(\alpha) \Gamma(1-\alpha)\sqrt{2\pi t(K+1)^3}}\int_0^1 \exp\left(-\frac{ts}{8}\right) s^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}(1-s)^{-\alpha}\, ds=\infty,
\end{multline*}
if $\alpha \le \frac{1}{2}$. Hence, in such a case, $q(t,\cdot)$ cannot admit a second order derivative in $x=K$. Suppose that we have already shown that $q$ is solution of (\ref{eq:BSUnder}), then this lack of parabolic smoothing is also common in time-nonlocal equations, see for instance \cite[Proposition 3.3]{ascione2024time}.
<!-- end rmk-6 -->
<!-- prop-12: body_md -->
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main2} it holds, for all $t,x>0$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_0^t |P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)|\, \nu_\alpha(ds)<\infty,
    \end{equation*}
    and, for all $x>0$ and $\lambda>\frac{3}{8}$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_0^{\infty}\int_0^t e^{-\lambda t}|P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)|\, \nu_\alpha(ds)\, dt<\infty
    \end{equation*}
    where $\nu_\alpha(ds)$ is the L\'evy measure corresponding to $\phi(\lambda) = \lambda^\alpha$, $\alpha \in (1/2,1)$, i.e., \begin{align}
        \nu_\alpha(ds)= \alpha s^{-\alpha-1}/\Gamma(1-\alpha)ds.
        \label{stablev}
    \end{align}
<!-- end prop-12 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
By Proposition \ref{prop:regq} we know that $q(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ hence we can write
\begin{equation*}
P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)=\int_0^s GP_\tau q(t,x) d\tau.
\end{equation*}
Recalling that, still by Proposition \ref{prop:regq}, $\partial_x q(t,\cdot), Gq(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$, by Item $(3)$ of Lemma \ref{lem:BSuniqueness} we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Psestq}
                                                 
{P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)=\int_0^s P_\tau G q(t,x) d\tau.}
\end{equation}
In particular, $Gq(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(1/2)$ and, by (\ref{eq:est3q})
\begin{equation*}
    [Gq(t,\cdot)]_{1/2} \le \sqrt{\frac{K}{2 t}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}.
\end{equation*}
Hence, by Item $(5)$ of Lemma \ref{lem:BSuniqueness} we get that $P_\tau Gq(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(1/2)$ with
\begin{equation*}
    [P_\tau Gq(t,\cdot)]_{1/2} \le \sqrt{\frac{2K}{ t}}e^{\frac{3}{8}\tau}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}.
\end{equation*}
Using this into (\ref{eq:Psestq}) we have
\begin{align*}
    \left|P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)\right|& {\le}\int_0^s \left|P_\tau Gvq(t,x)\right| d\tau \le \sqrt{\frac{2K}{ t}}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}\int_0^s e^{\frac{3}{8}\tau} \, d\tau\\
    &\le \sqrt{\frac{2K}{ t}}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}e^{\frac{3}{8}s}s.
\end{align*}
Integrating the latter against $\nu_\phi$, we have
\begin{align*}
\int_0^t \left|P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)\right|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}ds &\le \sqrt{\frac{2K}{ t}}e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}\int_0^t e^{\frac{3}{8}s}\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\, ds\\
&\le e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\alpha \Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right) \sqrt{2K}}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}e^{\frac{3}{8}t}t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}.
\end{align*}
Finally, integrating the latter against $e^{-\lambda t}$ for $\lambda>\frac{3}{8}$ we get
\begin{align*}
\int_0^{\infty} \int_0^t &e^{-\lambda t}\left|P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)\right|\frac{\alpha s^{-\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\, ds\, dt \\
&\le e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\alpha \Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{2K}}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\left(\lambda-\frac{3}{8}\right)t}t^{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}\, dt\\
&=e^{\frac{1}{2}|\log(x)|}\frac{\alpha \Gamma\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{2K}}{2\pi \Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\left(\lambda-\frac{3}{8}\right)^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\alpha\right).
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- prop-13: body_md -->
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main2} for all $x,\lambda>0$ it holds
    \begin{equation*}
    \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t} \overline{\nu}_\alpha(t) |q(t,x)|\, dt <\infty,
    \end{equation*}
    where $\bar{\nu}_\alpha(t)$ is the tail of (\ref{stablev}).
<!-- end prop-13 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Since $\overline{\nu}_\phi(t)=\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}$, we just use {the inequality} (\ref{eq:est1q}) to get
                   
                                                                                                                                                                                                                  
                 
    \textcolor{black}{\begin{align*}
    \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t} \frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)} |q(t,x)|\, dt \le \frac{e^{|\log(x)|}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty t^{-\alpha}e^{-\lambda t}dt=\frac{e^{|\log(x)|}}{\lambda^{1-\alpha}}.
    \end{align*}}
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- prop-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:qsolution}
Under the assumptions of Theorem \ref{thm:main2}, $q$ is a solution of (\ref{eq:BSUnder}).
<!-- end prop-14 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
In view of the results in Subsection \ref{regstar}, we know by Theorem \ref{thm:integrability2} that $(\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(t,x)$ is well-defined for all $x,t>0$. Moreover, for any $x>0$ we know by Theorem \ref{thm:Lapinside2} that $(\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(\cdot,x)$ admits Laplace transform for any $\lambda>\frac{3}{8}$.
    We divide the proof into two parts. First we check that for all $x>0$ and $\lambda>\frac{3}{8}$ we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Laptranseq}
\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}(\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(t,x)dt=\lambda^{\alpha-1}(x-K)_+,
\end{equation}
which is the first equation in  (\ref{eq:BSUnder}) after taking the Laplace transform in the $t$ variable on both sides. Then we use this to prove that
\begin{align}
    (\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(t,x) = \frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)} q(0,x).
\end{align}
We begin with (\ref{eq:Laptranseq}).
Consider $x>0$, $\lambda>\frac{3}{8}$ and denote
  \begin{equation*}
  \widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)=\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}q(t,x) \, dt.
  \end{equation*}
  Let us first evaluate an alternative formula for $\widetilde{q}$. Set $u_K(x)=(x-K)_+$ and notice that, by (\ref{eq:qspecial}), we get
    \begin{align}
    \widetilde{q}(\lambda,x) \, = \, &\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}\int_0^t s^{\alpha-1}(t-s)^{-\alpha}q_{\rm BS}(s,x)\, ds \, dt \notag \\
    = \, & \frac{\lambda^{\alpha -1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)} \int_0^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda s} s^{\alpha-1} q_{\rm BS}(s,x) \, ds \notag \\
    = \, & \frac{\lambda^{\alpha -1}}{\Gamma (\alpha)} \int_0^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda s} s^{\alpha -1} P_su_K(x) \, ds, \label{qtildastar}
    \end{align}
    where, in the first step, we used the convolution Theorem for Laplace transform (e.g., \cite[Proposition 1.6.4]{arendt2011vector}) and then we applied Tonelli's theorem since $q_{\rm BS}$ is non-negative.  As a consequence of (\ref{eq:postFubini}), we have that                                                                                                              
\begin{align}
		\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t} & \left(   \partial_t -G \right) ^\alpha q(x,t) \, dt \, \nonumber\\= \,
  & -\int_0^\infty \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t} (P_s q(t-s,x)\mathds{1}_{(0,t]}(s)-q(t,x))\, dt \,\nu_\alpha(ds) \nonumber\\
  \, = \, & -\int_0^\infty \left( \int_s^\infty  \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}q(t-s,y)p_{\rm GBM}(s,y;x)\, dy \, dt - \widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\right) \, \nu_\alpha (ds) \nonumber\\
  \, = \, & -\int_0^\infty \left( e^{-\lambda s}\int_0^\infty    \widetilde{q} (\lambda,y)p_{\rm GBM}(s,y;x)\, dy - \widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\right) \, \nu_\alpha (ds)\nonumber
  \\
  \, = \, & -\int_0^\infty \left( e^{-\lambda s}P_s(\widetilde{q} (\lambda,\cdot))(x) - \widetilde{q}(\lambda,x)\right) \, \nu_\alpha (ds), \nonumber
	\end{align}
 where in the third equality we used again Tonelli's theorem to exchange the order of the integrals (since $q$ and $p_{\rm GBM}$ are non-negative) after having made explicit the action of the operator $P_s$ in the second step.  Now we can replace $\widetilde{q}$ in the previous identity by making use of (\ref{qtildastar}). To do this, we first observe that
 \begin{align}
     P_s  \left(  \widetilde{q} (\lambda, \cdot) \right)  (x) \, = \, & \frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)} \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda \tau} \tau^{\alpha-1} P_\tau u_K(y) p_{\rm GBM}(s, y;x) d\tau \, dy \notag \\
     = \, & \frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)} \int_0^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda \tau} \tau^{\alpha-1} P_{s+\tau} u_K(x) \, d\tau
 \end{align}
 and then we have
 \begin{align}
		&\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}  \left(   \partial_t -G \right) ^\alpha q(x,t) \, dt \notag \\  = \, &-\frac{\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty   \int_0^{+\infty} \tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}\left( e^{-\lambda (s+\tau)} P_{s+\tau} u_K(x) \,  -   e^{-\lambda \tau} P_{\tau} u_K(x) \,  \right)d\tau\, ds \notag \\
  = \, & -\frac{\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty   \int_0^{+\infty} \tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda \tau}P_\tau\left( e^{-\lambda s} q_{\rm BS}(s,x)\,  -  u_K(x) \,  \right)d\tau\, ds,
  \end{align}
 where we used the fact that $P_\tau$ is a semigroup action and the explicit form of $\nu_\alpha(\cdot)$ given in (\ref{stablev}). However, if we observe that 
  \begin{align}
     e^{-\lambda s} q_{\rm BS}(s,x)\,  -  u_K(x) \, = \, & \int_0^s \partial_w e^{-\lambda w} q_{\rm BS} (w,x) \, dw \notag \\ \,& \int_0^s e^{-\lambda w}  \left(  \partial_w q_{\rm BS}(w,x) - \lambda q_{\rm BS} (w,x) \right)  \, dw \notag \\
     = \, & \int_0^s e^{-\lambda w}   \left(  G  - \lambda   \right)  \,  q_{\rm BS}(w,x) \, dw 
 \end{align}
and we explicitly write the integral in $P_\tau$, we get
 \begin{align}
     &\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}  \left(   \partial_t -G \right) ^\alpha q(x,t) \, dt \notag \\  = \, & -\frac{\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty   \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty} \int_0^s\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}  \left(  G  - \lambda   \right)  \,  q_{\rm BS}(w,y) \,  p_{\rm GBM}(\tau,y;x) \, dw  \, dy \, d\tau\, ds \label{eq:preFub}.
                                                                                                                                                                                                         
                                                                                                                                   
 \end{align}
                                          
We want to use Fubini's theorem to change the order of the integrals. To do this, we notice that (\ref{eq:der3}) implies $G q_{BS} \ge 0$ and then
 \begin{align}
 &   \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty} \int_0^s \tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)} \left| (G-\lambda) q_{\rm BS} (w,y) \right| p_{\rm GBM} (\tau, y;x) \, dw \, dy \, d\tau \, ds \notag \\ &\leq \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{s} \int_0^{+\infty} \tau^{\alpha-1} s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}(G+\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(w,y) p_{\rm GBM} (\tau, y;x) \, dy \, dw  \, d\tau \, ds\\
 &=\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{s} \tau^{\alpha-1} s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}P_\tau (G+\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(w,x) \, dw  \, d\tau \, ds
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      
                                                                                                                                                                                                       
 \label{bound646}
 \end{align}
Since $q_{\rm BS}(t,\cdot) \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$, $\partial_x q_{\rm BS} \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$ and $Gq_{\rm BS} \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0$, we can exchange the operator $(G+\lambda)$ with $P_\tau$, getting
 \begin{align}
 &   \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty} \int_0^s \tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)} \left| (G-\lambda) q_{\rm BS} (w,y) \right| p_{\rm GBM} (\tau, y;x) \, dw \, dy \, d\tau \, ds \notag \\ &\leq \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{s} \tau^{\alpha-1} s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}(G+\lambda)P_\tau q_{\rm BS}(w,x) \, dw  \, d\tau \, ds\\
 &= \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{s} \tau^{\alpha-1} s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}(G+\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x) \, dw  \, d\tau \, ds,
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      
                                                                                                                                                                                                       
 \label{bound6462}
 \end{align}
where we also used the fact that $P_\tau q_{\rm BS}(w,x)=P_\tau P_w u_K(x)=P_{\tau+w}u_K(x)=q_{\rm BS}(w+\tau,x)$. By (\ref{eq:der3}) we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:control}
    Gq_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x) \le \sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi(\tau+w)}}\exp\left(-\frac{\log^2\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)}{2(\tau+w)}\right).
\end{equation}
Consider the function $r \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \mapsto \sqrt{r}\exp\left(-r\frac{\log^2\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)}{2}\right) \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}$. It is possible to check that such a function has a maximum at $r_\star=\log^{-2}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)$ and then
\begin{equation*}
    \sqrt{r}\exp\left(-r\frac{\log^2\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)}{2}\right) \le \frac{1}{\sqrt{e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|.
\end{equation*}
Plugging the latter into (\ref{eq:control}), adding $\lambda q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x)$ and also using  (\ref{eq:der3.5}) we get
\begin{equation*}
    (G+\lambda)q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x) \le \sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|+\lambda e^{|\log(x)|}.
\end{equation*}
Plugging this inequality into (\ref{bound6462}) we obtain
 \begin{align}
&\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty} \int_0^s \tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)} \left| (G-\lambda) q_{\rm BS} (w,y) \right| p_{\rm GBM} (\tau, y;x) \, dw \, dy \, d\tau \, ds \notag \\ \leq \,& \frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|+\lambda e^{|\log(x)|}\right)\int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty}  \tau^{\alpha-1} (1-e^{-\lambda s})s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda \tau}  \, d\tau \, ds \nonumber\\
= & \frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|+\lambda e^{|\log(x)|}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda \tau} \, d\tau \right)\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}s^{-\alpha-1}(1-e^{-\lambda s}) \, ds\right)\nonumber\\
= &\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\sqrt{\frac{Kx}{8\pi e}}\left|\log^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{K}\right)\right|+\lambda e^{|\log(x)|}\right)\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{\alpha}<\infty \nonumber
\end{align}
where, in the last step, we used the integral representation of the Gamma function that
\begin{align*}
    \int_{0}^{+\infty}s^{-\alpha-1}(1-e^{-\lambda s}) \, ds = \frac{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{\alpha} \lambda^\alpha.
\end{align*}
We can now safely use Fubini's theorem in (\ref{eq:preFub}), obtaining
 \begin{align}
     &\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}  \left(   \partial_t -G \right) ^\alpha q(x,t) \, dt \notag \\  
                                                                                                                                                                                             
     &=  -\frac{\alpha\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty   \int_0^{+\infty}\int_0^{+\infty} \int_w^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}   \left(  G  - \lambda   \right)  \,  q_{\rm BS}(w,y) \,  p_{\rm GBM}(\tau,y;x) \, ds  \, dy \, d\tau\, dw \nonumber \\
     &=  -\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty   \int_0^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}w^{-\alpha}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}  P_\tau  \left(  G  - \lambda   \right)  \,  q_{\rm BS}(w,x)   \, d\tau\, dw \notag\\
     &=  -\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty   \int_0^{+\infty}\tau^{\alpha-1}w^{-\alpha}e^{-\lambda (\tau+w)}    \left(  G  - \lambda   \right)  \,  q_{\rm BS}(\tau+w,x)   \, d\tau\, dw \notag \\
     &=  -\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty   \int_w^{+\infty}(z-w)^{\alpha-1}w^{-\alpha}    \left(  G  - \lambda   \right)  \,  e^{-\lambda z} q_{\rm BS}(z,x)   \, dz\, dw \notag\\
     &=  -\frac{\lambda^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^\infty    \left(  G  - \lambda   \right)  \,  e^{-\lambda z} q_{\rm BS}(z,x)\left(\int_0^{z}(z-w)^{\alpha-1}w^{-\alpha}   \, dw\right)\, dz \notag\\
     &= -\lambda^{\alpha-1}\int_0^\infty    \left(  G  - \lambda   \right)  \,  e^{-\lambda z} q_{\rm BS}(z,x) dz, \label{eq:tosubs}
 \end{align}
where in the third equality we used that $P_\tau q_{\rm BS} (w,y) = P_\tau P_w u_K(x) = P_{\tau+w}u_K(x) = q_{\rm BS} (\tau+w,x)$, in the fourth equality we used the change of variables $\tau=z-w$, in the fifth equality we used again Fubini's theorem and in the last equality we used the integral representation of the Beta function. Now notice that (\ref{eq:der3.5}), (\ref{eq:der4}) and (\ref{eq:der5}) guarantee that we can take the Laplace transform in the variable $t$ on both sides of the first equation of (\ref{eq:BSclass1}), leading to
\begin{equation*}
\lambda \int_0^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda z}q_{\rm BS}(z,x)dz-u_K(x)=\int_0^{+\infty} e^{-\lambda z}Gq_{\rm BS}(z,x)dz.
\end{equation*}
This equality can be rearranged to obtain
\begin{equation*}
\int_0^{+\infty} (\lambda-G)e^{-\lambda z} q_{\rm BS}(z,x)dz=u_K(x)
\end{equation*}
which, replaced into (\ref{eq:tosubs}) gives (\ref{eq:Laptranseq}).

By injectivity of the Laplace transform, we know that for all $x>0$ there exists a measurable set $\mathcal{I}_x \subset \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+$ such that $|\mathcal{I}_x|=0$ and for all $t \in \operatorname{\mathbb{R}}^+ \setminus \mathcal{I}_x$ one has
\begin{equation*}
    (\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(t,x)=\frac{t^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}q(0,x).
\end{equation*}
We want to prove that $\mathcal{I}_x=\varnothing$ for all $x>0$. To do this, we first show that $q$ is continuous. Indeed, let $t,x>0$ and consider a sequence $(t_n,x_n) \to (t,x)$. By (\ref{eq:qspecial}) and (\ref{eq:selfsim}) we have
\begin{equation*}
    q(t_n,x_n)=\int_0^1 q_{\rm BS}(t_ns,x_n)\frac{(1-s)^{-\alpha}s^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds.
\end{equation*}
Since $x_n \to x$, there exist $0<a<b$ such that $x_n \in [a,b]$ for all $n \in \operatorname{\mathbb{N}}$ and then $|q_{\rm BS}(t_ns,x_n)| \le e^{\max\{|\log(a)|, |\log(b)|\}}$. Hence, we can use the dominated convergence theorem to get
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{n \to +\infty}q(t_n,x_n)=\int_0^1 q_{\rm BS}(ts,x)\frac{(1-s)^{-\alpha}s^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)\Gamma(\alpha)}ds=q(t,x).
\end{equation*}
Now we prove that for fixed $x>0$ the function $(\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(\cdot,x)$ is right-continuous. Indeed, let $t_n \downarrow t$ for some $t>0$ and observe that 
\begin{align*}
    &|(\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(t_n,x)-(\partial_t-G)^\alpha q(t,x)| \\
    &\le \frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_0^t |P_sq(t-s,x)-q(t,x)-P_sq(t_n-s,x)+q(t_n,x)| s^{-1-\alpha}ds\\
    &+\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_t^{t_n} |P_sq(t_n-s,x)-q(t_n,x)| s^{-1-\alpha}ds\\
    &+\frac{t^{-\alpha}-t_n^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}|q(t,x)|+|q(t,x)-q(t_n,x)|\frac{t_n^{-\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\\
    &:=\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\left(I_n(x)+J_n(x)+\frac{t^{-\alpha}-t_n^{-\alpha}}{\alpha}|q(t,x)|+|q(t,x)-q(t_n,x)|\frac{t_n^{-\alpha}}{\alpha}\right).
\end{align*}
Let us first consider $I_n(x)$. We have
\begin{align*}
    |P_sq(t-s,x)&-q(t,x)-P_sq(t_n-s,x)+q(t_n,x)|\\
    & \le |P_sq(t-s,x)-P_sq(t_n-s,x)| + |q(t,x)-q(t_n,x)|\\
    &\le P_s|q(t_n-s,\cdot)-q(t-s,\cdot)|(x) + |q(t,x)-q(t_n,x)|
\end{align*}
Arguing as in the proof of Corollary \ref{coro:diffest}, we have that $|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t_n-s,\cdot)| \in \operatorname{\mathcal{C}}_0(1/2)$ with $t_n-s-(t_n-t)$
\begin{equation*}
[|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t_n-s,\cdot)|]_{1/2} \le \frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)(t_n-t)}{\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{K}{t-s}}.
\end{equation*}
Hence, by Item $(5)$ of Lemma \ref{lem:BSuniqueness} we have
\begin{align*}
P_s|q(t_n-s,\cdot)-q(t-s,\cdot)|(x) \le 2\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)(t_n-t)}{\pi \Gamma(\alpha)}\sqrt{\frac{K}{t-s}}e^{\frac{3}{8}s+\frac{|\log(x)|}{2}}.
\end{align*}
Combining this inequality with the fact that $q$ is continuous, for fixed $s \in [0,t)$, we find
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{n \to \infty}|P_sq(t-s,x)-q(t,x)-P_sq(t_n-s,x)+q(t_n,x)|=0.
\end{equation*}
Hence, it is sufficient to show that we can use the dominated convergence theorem to take the limit inside the integral sign in $I_n(x)$. To do this, observe that
\begin{align}\label{eq:In0}
\begin{split}
    |P_sq(t-s,x)&-q(t,x)-P_sq(t_n-s,x)+q(t_n,x)|\\
    & \le |P_sq(t-s,x)-P_sq(t,x)| + |P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)|\\
    & \qquad +|P_sq(t_n-s,x)-P_sq(t_n,x)|+|P_sq(t_n,x)-q(t_n,x)|\\
    &\le P_s|q(t-s,\cdot)-q(t,\cdot)|(x) + |P_sq(t,x)-q(t,x)|\\
    & \qquad + P_s|q(t_n-s,\cdot)-q(t_n,\cdot)|(x)+|P_sq(t_n,x)-q(t_n,x)|.
    \end{split}
